New Formula for Entropy Solutions for Scalar Hyperbolic Conservation Laws with Flux Functions of Convexity Degeneracy and Global Dynamic Patterns of Solutions
Gaowei Cao, Gui-Qiang G. Chen †, Xiaozhou Yang

Abstract
. We are concerned with a new solution formula and its applications to the analysis of properties of entropy solutions of the Cauchy problem for one-dimensional scalar hyperbolic conservation laws, wherein the flux functions exhibit convexity degeneracy and the initial data are in L ∞ L^{\infty} . We first introduce and validate the novel formula for entropy solutions for the Cauchy problem, which generalizes the Lax–Oleinik formula. Then, by employing this formula, we obtain a series of fine properties of entropy solutions and discover several new structures and phenomena, which are summarized in the following three aspects: (i) Series of results on the fine structures of entropy solutions, especially including the new criteria for all six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem, the new structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangle, and the new features of the formation and development of shocks such as all five types of continuous shock generation points, along with their criteria and the optimal regularities of the corresponding resulting shocks; (ii) Series of results on the global structures of entropy solutions, including the four new invariants of entropy solutions, the new criteria for the locations and speeds of divides, and the exact determination of the global structures of entropy solutions; (iii) Series of new results on the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions, including the asymptotic profiles and decay rates of entropy solutions for initial data in L ∞ L^{\infty} , respectively in the L ∞ L^{\infty} –norm and the L loc p L^{p}_{{\rm loc}} –norm: The asymptotic profile in the L ∞ L^{\infty} –norm is either a rarefaction-constant solution (as a function only consisting of some constant regions, centered rarefaction waves, and/or rarefaction regions) or a single shock, and the asymptotic profile in the L loc p L^{p}_{{\rm loc}} –norm is the generalized N N –wave as we introduce. Through the above results (i)–(iii), we obtain the global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions for scalar hyperbolic conservation laws with the flux functions satisfying ( 1.3 ) and general initial data in L ∞ L^{\infty} . Moreover, the new solution formula is also extended to more general scalar hyperbolic conservation laws.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35L65,35L67,35C99,35L03,35B65.

1.
Introduction

We are concerned with
a new solution formula
and its applications to the analysis of properties of entropy solutions of
the Cauchy problem for
one-dimensional scalar hyperbolic conservation laws:

(1.1)

\displaystyle u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}:=({-}\infty,\infty)\times(0,\infty)$},

(1.2)

\displaystyle u|_{t=0}=\varphi(x),

wherein the initial data function
\varphi(x)
is in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and the flux function
f(u)
exhibits convexity degeneracy:

(1.3)

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 0\,\,\,\,\mbox{on $u\in\mathbb{R}$},\qquad\,\,\,\mathcal{L}\{u\,:\,f^{\prime\prime}(u)=0\}=0,

where
\mathcal{L}
is the Lebesgue measure.

As is well-known, due to the nonlinearity of the flux function
f(u)
,
no matter how smooth the initial data function
\varphi(x)
is,
the solution may develop singularities and form shock waves (shocks, for short)
generically in a finite time,
so that entropy solutions must be considered.
We are concerned with not only the existence and uniqueness,
but also, more importantly, research for a more general solution formula
and its applications to the analysis of properties of entropy solutions
so as to establish the global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions.
This has been shown
to be also helpful for understanding
and analyzing
some important hyperbolic systems of conservation laws
(see, for example,
[
35
,
36
,
59
,
61
]
)
and related stochastic partial differential equations
(see, for example,
[
31
,
27
,
53
]
), among others.

Owing to the nonlinearity of the flux functions
and the complicated interaction of characteristics,
representative formulas for general entropy solutions
have been
restricted to the case of the one-dimensional scalar conservation laws
with a uniformly convex flux function only.
The first successful attempt of solution formulas was given by Hopf
[
34
]
,
in which the solution formula for the inviscid Burgers equation
was obtained by the vanishing viscosity limit.
For equation
(
1.1
)
with flux functions
f(u)
satisfying that

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
,
Lax
[
39
]
gave the implicit representation for entropy solutions
and Oleinik
[
49
]
gave a formula for entropy solutions
in a different way.
However, for the general case
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 0
,
there is no representation formula of entropy solutions available,
which is one of our main motivations of this paper.

For properties of entropy solutions of
the scalar conservation law
(
1.1
)
,
Lax
[
39
,
40
]
gave the invariants of entropy solutions
for initial data in
L^{\infty}\cap L^{1}

and obtained the development of
N
–waves for initial data with compact support
and the formation of the sawtooth profiles for periodic initial data.
Dafermos
[
19
,
20
,
21
]
established the method of generalized characteristics
and obtained many confinement structures and several
cases of asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions.
Quinn
[
52
]
noted the
L^{1}
–contraction property for piecewise smooth solutions,
Holden–Holden
[
30
]
proved the
L^{1}
–stability via the numerical method
introduced by Dafermos
[
18
]
,
and Holden–Risebro–Tveito
[
33
]
proved that the maximum principle holds
for weak solutions as limits of approximate solutions generated by the Lax–Friedrichs scheme,
the Godunov scheme,
and the Glimm scheme (even for the case of hyperbolic systems).
Moreover,
Lin
[
44
]
gave a description of all the six types of
initial waves for the Cauchy problem
but did not obtain the concrete criteria,
Huang
[
35
]
gave a description of the backward characteristic triangles and the shock curves,
Lebaud
[
41
]
and Yin–Zhu
[
62
]
proved that the singularity
around the first blowup point caused by the initial compression is actually a shock
under some specific degenerate information of sufficiently smooth initial data functions,
Li–Wang
[
43
]
gave the global structures of entropy solutions
for several special types of the
L^{\infty}
initial data functions,
and Li
[
42
]
gave a description of the global structures of shocks.
Furthermore, Liu
[
46
]
gave the stability of Riemann solutions
under the compact perturbation
and Chen–Frid
[
12
,
13
]
showed the large-time stability of the Riemann solutions
with respect to the
L^{\infty}\cap L^{1}
perturbation
and the large-time decay of periodic entropy solutions
of hyperbolic conservation laws (even for the multidimensional case).
However, many important properties of entropy solutions have not yet been explored and/or understood
when the uniform convexity of the flux function of
(
1.1
)
fails, especially
for the global dynamic patterns of the entropy solutions.
See also Dafermos
[
22
]

and the references cited therein.

The scalar hyperbolic conservation laws, serving as the foundational hyperbolic equations,
represent a crucial starting point for the analysis of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws.
Moreover, numerous ideas, concepts, and methodologies gleaned from analyzing scalar hyperbolic
conservation laws have played pivotal roles in advancing the theory
of hyperbolic systems of conservation laws. As examples, these especially include
the minimal entropy conditions
(
cf.
Panov
[
50
]
, Bouchut–Perthame
[
6
]
,
De Lellis–Otto–Westdickenberg
[
24
]
,
Cao–Chen
[
11
]
, and Krupa–Vasseur
[
37
]
),
the kinetic formulations
(
cf.
Lions–Perthame–Tadmor
[
45
]
,
De Lellis–Otto–Westdickenberg
[
23
]
,
Tadmor–Tao
[
58
]
, and Golse–Perthame
[
28
]
),
the entropy dissipation
(
cf.
De Lellis–Riviere
[
25
]
, Bianchini–Marconi
[
4
,
48
]
,
and Silvestre
[
57
]
),
the BV/SBV and fractional BV
(
cf.
Ambrosio–De Lellis
[
2
]
, Bourdarias–Gisclon–Junca
[
7
]
,
Bianchini–Yu
[
5
]
, and Marconi
[
47
]
),
the divergence-measure fields and normal traces
(
cf.
Chen–Frid
[
14
]
, Chen–Rascle
[
17
]
, Chen–Perthame
[
16
]
,
Vasseur
[
60
]
, Kwon–Vasseur
[
38
]
, and Bürger–Frid–Karlsen
[
10
]
),
the contractive semigroups
(
cf.
Quinn
[
52
]
, Andreianov–Karlsen–Risebro
[
3
]
),
Serre–Silvestre
[
56
]
, and Serre
[
54
,
55
]
),
the front-tracking method
(
cf.
Dafermos
[
18
]
, DiPerna
[
26
]
,
Bressan
[
8
]
, and Bressan–Liu–Yang
[
9
]
),
among others.
Also see
Ambrosio–Crippa–De Lellis–Otto–Westdickenberg
[
1
]
,
Chen–Holden–Karlsen
[
15
]
,
Holden–Risebro
[
32
]
,
Perthame
[
51
]
,
and the references cited therein.

For the reasons discussed above,
achieving the global dynamic patterns
of the entropy solutions to scalar hyperbolic conservation laws
is paramount for advancing the theory of hyperbolic conservation laws.
This paper is dedicated to fulfilling this purpose.
One of the motivations of this paper
that both formulas for entropy solutions given by Lax
[
40
]
and Oleinik
[
49
]

are not suitable for many convex functions
f(u)
,
such as the
typical flux functions
f(u)=u^{2n}
with
n>1
and
f(u)=e^{ku}
with
k\neq 0
.
Another motivation is that the complete description of
many properties of entropy solutions has not been achieved
before,
even when the flux function is uniformly convex;
these include the following aspects:
The criteria for initial waves of the Cauchy problem,
the general cases of the formation and development of shocks,
the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles,
the necessary and sufficient conditions for the locations and speeds of divides,
and the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions with general initial data in
L^{\infty}
.
Our third motivation is that there are very few results
on the regularity and asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions
when the convex flux function possesses
the state points of convexity degeneracy and/or asymptotic lines.

The aim of this paper is twofold:
Firstly, we introduce and validate a novel formula for entropy solutions
of scalar hyperbolic conservation laws with
the flux functions satisfying
(
1.3
)

(
i.e.
, the uniform convexity is not required);
this new formula aligns with the well-known Lax–Oleinik formula
[
39
,
40
,
49
]

in the case that the flux function is of uniform convexity.
Importantly, the method used to derive this new formula is rooted in the understanding of
the intrinsic formula for smooth solutions (see Remark
2.5
),
offering a new approach to identifying formulas
for entropy solutions in more general hyperbolic conservation laws
where such intrinsic formulas are known.
Indeed, as shown in §8,
the new formula for entropy solutions can be extended to
more general scalar hyperbolic conservation laws
(
8.1
)

with the flux pair
(U(u),F(u))
satisfying
(
8.3
)

and the initial data in
L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}
satisfying
(
8.4
)
.
Secondly, we
leverage this newly derived formula to elucidate various fine properties of entropy solutions
and obtain the global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions
for the Cauchy problem with

flux functions satisfying
(
1.3
)
and initial data only
in
L^{\infty}
;
these especially include:

(i)

Criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
and dynamic behaviors of characteristics in
§3
.

We prove the necessary and sufficient conditions
for the rays emitting from points on the
x
–axis to be characteristics at least local-in-time,
and obtain the criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
by providing the necessary and sufficient conditions for all the six
types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
even when the point on the
x
–axis is not a Lebesgue point of the initial data function.
Furthermore, we introduce and prove the lifespan of characteristics,
and provide a classification of characteristics by the way of their terminations.

(ii)

New features of the formation and development of shocks in
§4

and fine structures of entropy solutions in
§5
.

In §4.1, we discover all the five types of continuous shock generation points
and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions of them;
in §4.2, we prove the optimal regularities of shock curves and entropy solutions
near all the five types of continuous shock generation points,
which are not necessarily assumed to be isolated.
In §5, the fine structures of entropy solutions are established
by providing and proving the new structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles,
proving the directional limits of entropy solutions at the discontinuous points
(even for the points at which there are infinitely many shocks collided together)
and the general structures of shocks (especially including the left-derivatives of them).

(iii)

Four new invariants, criteria for divides as well as their locations and speeds, and the exact determination of global structures of entropy solutions in
§6
.

We first introduce and prove the four invariants of entropy solutions,
and then provide and prove
the necessary and sufficient conditions respectively for the divides as well as their locations and speeds.
Furthermore, by using the results on invariants and divides,
the global structures of entropy solutions are obtained by proving
all the path-connected branches of the shock set of entropy solutions,
which are actually separated by the
nearby
divides.

(iv)

Asymptotic profiles and decay rates respectively
in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm in
§7
.

Using the advantages of the results on invariants and divides
developed in this paper,
we prove that the entropy solutions decay to either a rarefaction-constant solution
(as a function only consisting of some constant regions,
centered rarefaction waves, and/or rarefaction regions)
or a single shock in the
L^{\infty}
–norm,
and obtain the corresponding decay rates.
Moreover, introducing the concept of generalized
N
–waves, we prove that
the entropy solutions, which possess at least one divide,
decay to the generalized
N
–waves in the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm,
and obtain the corresponding decay rates.

(v)

Influences of the convexity degeneracy of flux functions.

As shown in this paper, the convexity degeneracy of the flux function
f(u)

(
i.e.
, the infinitesimal order of
f^{\prime\prime}(u)
at degeneracy point)
influences the behaviors of entropy solutions substantially,
especially including the generation of characteristics and shocks,
the formation and development of shocks,
and the decay rates respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm.

The global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions
with
general initial data in
L^{\infty}
are new,
even for the case when the flux function
f(u)
is uniformly convex;
these especially include: The criteria for all the six types
of initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles,
the directional limits of entropy solutions at the discontinuous points
and the left-derivatives of shock curves,
the formation and development of shocks for all the five types of continuous shock generation points,
the four invariants,
the necessary and sufficient conditions respectively for the divides as well as their locations and speeds,
the exact determination of the global structures of entropy solutions,
and the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions
respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{\rm loc}
–norm.
Our proofs are based on the new formula for entropy solutions
of the Cauchy problem with flux functions allowing for convexity degeneracy and initial data only in
L^{\infty}
.

Finally, we extend the new formula for entropy solutions to more general scalar hyperbolic conservation laws
U(u)_{t}+F(u)_{x}=0
in §8,
where the flux pair
(U(u),F(u))
satisfies
(
8.3
)

and the initial data function in
L^{1}_{{\rm loc}}
satisfies
(
8.4
)
.
The corresponding fine properties of entropy solutions are also presented.

2.
New Formula for Entropy Solutions and Its Main
Application Results

In this section, we first introduce a new formula
for entropy solutions of
the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
with
the flux function
f(u)
satisfying
(
1.3
)
,
and then validate it by proving that the function
defined by this newly derived formula
solves the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
uniquely
in Theorem
2.4
.
As the direct applications of this newly derived formula,
the refined
L^{1}-
contraction
and some pointwise properties such as the monotonicity in
L^{\infty}

are proved in Corollary
2.5
,
and the refined semigroup properties
are proved in Corollary
2.6
.

In §2.2,
we present a summary of the main results on the fine properties of entropy solutions,
which are elucidated in §3–§7
via leveraging this newly derived formula.

We first state the notions of weak solutions and entropy solutions, respectively.

Definition 2.1

(Weak Solutions)
.

A bounded measurable function
u=u(x,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
is called
to be a weak solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
if
u=u(x,t)
satisfies

(2.1)

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\big(u\phi_{t}+f(u)\phi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=0\qquad\,\,\,\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ \phi=\phi(x,t)\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0{+}}\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}u(x,t)\,{\rm d}x=\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}\varphi(x)\,{\rm d}x\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}.

Definition 2.2

(Entropy Solutions)
.

A weak solution
u=u(x,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
is called
to be
an entropy solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)

if it satisfies the Oleinik-type one-sided inequality
:

(2.3)

f^{\prime}(u(x_{2},t))-f^{\prime}(u(x_{1},t))\leq\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}}{t}\qquad\,\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ x_{2}>x_{1}\ {\rm and}\ t>0.

2.1.
New solution formula and its direct corollaries

We first introduce the new formula for entropy solutions.

For any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
we define a function
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
in variable
u\in\mathbb{R}
by

(2.4)

E(u;x,t):=t\int_{0}^{u}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\,{\rm d}s.

Without loss of generality,
we set
f(0)=0
.

From
(
1.3
)
,
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing.
Then, for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))<s\,\,\,\,{\rm if}\ s>\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\qquad\,\,\,\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))>s\,\,\,\,{\rm if}\ s<-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Thus, by
(
1.3
)
,
if
s>\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}
, then

f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\leq 0

so that
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
is strictly decreasing;
and, if
s<-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}
, then

f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\geq 0

so that
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
is strictly increasing.
Therefore, for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

E(\cdot\,;x,t)
always attains its maximum in the interval

[-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}]
.

Let
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
be the set of points at which
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum:

(2.5)

\mathcal{U}(x,t):=\big\{w\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,E(w;x,t)=\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}}E(v;x,t)\big\}.

Then, by the continuity of
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
,
\,\mathcal{U}(x,t)
is a bounded closed set
and
\mathcal{U}(x,t)\neq\varnothing
.

Definition 2.3
.

We define
u^{-}(x,t)
and
u^{+}(x,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

(2.6)

u^{-}(x,t)=\sup\,\mathcal{U}(x,t),\qquad\,\,\,u^{+}(x,t)=\inf\,\mathcal{U}(x,t).

Since
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
is closed,
then
u^{\pm}(x,t)\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
(see Fig.
2.1
).
For any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, let

(2.7)

\quad\hat{E}(x,t):=E(u^{\pm}(x,t);x,t)-\int_{0}^{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi=\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}}E(v;x,t)-\int_{0}^{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi.

Figure 2.1.
u^{\pm}(x,t)\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)\subset[u^{+}(x,t),u^{-}(x,t)]
.

We now present the main theorem on the new formula for entropy solutions.

Theorem 2.4

(New formula for entropy solutions)
.

Let
f(u)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be a convex flux function of
(
1.1
)
satisfying
(
1.3
)
,
and let the initial data function
\varphi(x)
be in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
u=u(x,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
be defined by

(2.8)

u(x,t):=u^{+}(x,t)=\inf\big\{w\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,E(w;x,t)=\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}}E(v;x,t)\big\}

with
E(u;x,t)
given by
(
2.4
)
.
Then
(
2.8
)
is the solution formula of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
)
in the following sense
:

u=u(x,t)
, defined by
(
2.8
)
,
is the unique entropy solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
.

Furthermore, the left- and right-traces of
u=u(x,t)
exist pointwise and satisfy

(2.9)

u(x{-},t)=u^{-}(x,t),\qquad u(x{+},t)=u^{+}(x,t)\qquad{\rm on}\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+};

moreover, for each
t>0
,

u(x-,t)=u(x+,t)
holds except at most a countable set of
(x,t)
at which
u(x-,t)>u(x+,t)
, and

(2.10)

f^{\prime}(u(\cdot,t))\in{\rm BV}_{{\rm loc}}(\mathbb{R})\qquad\,\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ t>0.

Remark 2.1
.

Theorem
2.4
still holds if the solution is alternatively defined by

u(x,t):=u^{-}(x,t)=\sup\big\{w\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,E(w;x,t)=\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}}E(v;x,t)\big\}.

In fact, as shown in Theorem
2.4
,
we can prove that

u(x-,t)=u(x+,t)
holds except at most a countable set of
(x,t)
for each
t>0
,
then it follows from
(
2.9
)
that

u^{-}(x,t)=u^{+}(x,t)
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}

(see also Lemma
2.7
).

Corollary 2.5
.

The entropy solutions, given by the solution formula
(
2.8
)
,
satisfy the following properties
:

(i)

Let
u_{1}(x,t)
and
u_{2}(x,t)
as in
(
2.8
)
be determined by
E(u;x,t)
in
(
2.4
)

with the
L^{\infty}
initial data functions
\varphi_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{2}(x)
, respectively.
Then, for any
t>0
and
x_{2}>x_{1}
,

(2.11)

\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}|u_{2}(x,t)-u_{1}(x,t)|\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(M(x_{1},t))}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(m(x_{2},t))}|\varphi_{2}(x)-\varphi_{1}(x)|\,{\rm d}x,

where
M(x,t):=\max\{u_{1}(x{-},t),u_{2}(x{-},t)\}
and
m(x,t):=\min\{u_{1}(x{+},t),u_{2}(x{+},t)\}
.

(ii)

Let
u_{i}(x,t)
as in
(
2.8
)
be determined by
E(u;x,t)
in
(
2.4
)

with the
L^{\infty}
initial data functions
\varphi_{i}(x)
for
i=1,2
.
If
\varphi_{1}(x)\leq\varphi_{2}(x)
holds almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}
, then

(2.12)

u_{1}(x\pm,t)\leq u_{2}(x\pm,t)\qquad\,\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

(iii)

Let
u_{n}(x,t)
and
u(x,t)
as in
(
2.8
)
be determined by
E(u;x,t)
in
(
2.4
)

with the
L^{\infty}
initial data functions
\varphi_{n}(x)
and
\varphi(x)
, respectively.
If
\{\varphi_{n}(x)\}_{n}
is an increasing sequence such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varphi_{n}-\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
, then

(2.13)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}(x+,t)=u(x+,t)\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+};

and if
\{\varphi_{n}(x)\}_{n}
is a decreasing sequence such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varphi_{n}-\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
, then

(2.14)

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}(x-,t)=u(x-,t)\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ (x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Remark 2.2
.

In Corollary
2.5
,
the inequality in
(
2.11
)
provides a refined
L^{1}
–contraction property
with the exact upper and lower limits of the integral on the
x
–axis;
the inequalities in
(
2.12
)
provide a refined monotonicity in
L^{\infty}

which holds pointwise, instead of almost everywhere;
and the limits in
(
2.13
)
–
(
2.14
)
provide
the pointwise convergence of entropy solutions
for the monotone sequence of the initial data,
which are not found in previous literature.

For any fixed
\tau>0
, consider the following Cauchy problem:

(2.15)

\begin{cases}v_{t}+f(v)_{x}=0\qquad&{\rm for}\ x\in\mathbb{R},\ t>\tau,\\[2.84526pt]
v(x,t)|_{t=\tau}=u(x,\tau)\qquad&{\rm for}\ x\in\mathbb{R},\ t=\tau,\end{cases}

where
u(x,\tau)
is obtained by restricting
u(x,t)
in
(
2.8
)
to the line:
t=\tau
.

Similar to
(
2.4
)
,
for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
t>\tau
, we define

(2.16)

E(v;x,t;\tau):=(t-\tau)\int_{0}^{v}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(u(x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(s),\tau)-s\big)\,{\rm d}s,

and similar to
(
2.5
)
–
(
2.7
)
,

(2.17)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau):=\big\{v\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,E(v;x,t;\tau)=\max_{w\in\mathbb{R}}E(w;x,t;\tau)\big\},\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle v^{-}(x,t):=\sup\,\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau),\qquad v^{+}(x,t):=\inf\,\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau),\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,t;\tau):=\max_{w\in\mathbb{R}}E(w;x,t;\tau)+\hat{E}(x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(0),\tau).\end{cases}

By Theorem
2.4
,

v(x,t):=v^{+}(x,t)
is the unique entropy solution of the Cauchy problem
(
2.15
)
.
In fact, we have stronger results as follows:

Corollary 2.6

(Refined semigroup properties of entropy solutions determined by the new solution formula)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
and
\hat{E}(x,t)
be defined by
(
2.5
)
and
(
2.7
)
respectively,
and let
\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)
and
\hat{E}(x,t;\tau)
for
t>\tau>0

be defined by
(
2.17
)
.
Then,
for any
(x,t)
with
t>\tau
,

(2.18)

\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)=\mathcal{U}(x,t),\qquad\hat{E}(x,t;\tau)=\hat{E}(x,t).

In particular,
v(x,t)
satisfies that

(2.19)

v(x\pm,t)=u(x\pm,t)\qquad\,\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ x\in\mathbb{R},\ t>\tau,

and the map

S_{t}:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\times[0,\infty)\rightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, associated with

S_{t}\varphi=u(\cdot,t)
for
u(x,0)=\varphi(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}),

determines a contractive semigroup in the
L^{1}_{\rm loc}
–norm.

Remark 2.3
.

As shown in Corollary
2.6
,

(
2.19
)
implies the semigroup property of entropy solutions,
i.e.
,
for any
\varphi(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,

S_{t}\varphi=S_{t-\tau}S_{\tau}\varphi
for
\tau\in[0,t].

Moreover, since
(
2.19
)
holds pointwise,
it can infer the fine properties of entropy solutions more than the semigroup property.
For examples,
by the arbitrariness of
\tau>0
in
(
2.15
)
,
Corollary
2.6

(especially the equality:
v(x\pm,t)=u(x\pm,t)
in
(
2.19
)
)
is a powerful tool to prove the local structures of entropy solutions; in particular,
it has been used to establish
the development of shocks near all the five types of continuous shock generation
points in Theorem
4.4
and prove the left- and
right-derivatives of shock curves in Theorem 5.5.

2.2.
Main results on fine properties of entropy solutions via the solution formula

In §3–§7,
we leverage the newly derived solution formula

(
2.8
)
with
E(u;x,t)
given by
(
2.4
)

to elucidate the fine properties of entropy solutions, and
obtain the global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions
for the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
with

flux functions allowing for convexity degeneracy and initial data only in
L^{\infty}
,
which can be summarized in four aspects:

1. Global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions:

Initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
fine structures of entropy solutions,
invariants and global structures of entropy solutions,
and asymptotic behaviors and decay rates
respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{\rm loc}
–norm.

(i)

Generation of initial waves for the Cauchy problem.

The criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
are established by proving the necessary and sufficient conditions
for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
in Theorem
3.9
associating with Theorem
3.8
,
which includes the case that the point is not a Lebesgue point of the initial data function
(
cf.
Fig.
2.2
).

Figure 2.2.
Initial waves for the Cauchy problem emitting from point
x_{0}

when
a=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=b.

For such four cases, the initial waves
are the combination of a rarefaction wave with/without one or two shocks.

(ii)

Fine structures of entropy solutions.

The fine structures of entropy solutions are obtained
by proving the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles in Theorem
5.3
,
the directional limits of entropy solutions at discontinuous points in Theorem
5.4
,
the general structures of shocks in Theorem
5.5
,
and the fine structures of entropy solutions on the shock sets in Theorem
5.6
(
cf.
Fig.
2.3
).

Figure 2.3.
Structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles
from discontinuous points.
In each
\Delta_{I_{m}}
, there is a centered compression wave;
and, in each
\Delta_{J_{n}}
, any two shocks must coincide each other before time
t_{0}
.

(iii)

Invariants and global structures of entropy solutions.

The four invariants of entropy solutions, including
\overline{u}_{l}
and
\underline{u}_{r}
,
are introduced and proved in Theorem
6.2
.
Using the results on invariants and divides developed in this paper,
the global structures of entropy solutions are obtained in Theorem
6.12

by proving all the path-connected branches of the shock set of entropy solutions
(see Figs.
2.4
–
2.6
).

(iv)

Asymptotic profiles and decay rates in the
L^{\infty}
–norm.

The asymptotic profiles of entropy solutions in the
L^{\infty}
–norm are proved to be
either a rarefaction-constant solution or a single shock
in Theorem
7.3
with Lemmas
7.1
–
7.2
,
and the corresponding decay rates are obtained
in Theorems
7.4
–
7.5

(see Figs.
2.4
–
2.6
).

Figure 2.4.
Divides, global structures of entropy solutions,
and asymptotic profiles in the
L^{\infty}
–norm
when
\overline{u}_{l}<\underline{u}_{r}
.
The divides form isolated characteristics,
centered rarefaction waves, and/or rarefaction regions;
and, in each of the
parallelogram regions,
any two shocks must coincide each other in a finite time.

Figure 2.5.
Divides, global structures of entropy solutions,
and asymptotic profiles in the
L^{\infty}
–norm
when
\overline{u}_{l}=\underline{u}_{r}
with
\mathcal{K}_{0}\neq\varnothing
.
Every divide possesses the same speed
f^{\prime}(\underline{u}_{r})
;
and, in each of the remaining parallelogram regions, any two shocks must coincide each other in a finite time.

Figure 2.6.
Divides, global structures of entropy solutions,
and asymptotic profiles in the
L^{\infty}
–norm
when
\overline{u}_{l}>\underline{u}_{r}
or

\overline{u}_{l}=\underline{u}_{r}
with
\mathcal{K}_{0}=\varnothing
.
Any two shocks in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
must coincide each other in a finite time
(for this case, the entropy solution possesses no divides).

(v)

Asymptotic profiles and decay rates in the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm.

Introducing the generalized
N
–waves in Definition
7.7
,
we prove that the entropy solutions, which possess at least one divide,
decay to the generalized
N
–waves in the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm
in Lemma
7.8
and Theorem
7.9
,
in which the corresponding decay rates are also obtained
(see Fig.
2.7
).

Figure 2.7.
Generalized
N
–waves as the asymptotic profiles in the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm.
The generalized
N
–wave is defined in the form of

w(x,t)=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(\frac{x-\xi}{t})

for
\xi
belonging to the closed set of the divide generation points on the
x
–axis.

2. Dynamic behaviors of characteristics:
The generation, spread, and termination of characteristics;
and the divides as well as their locations and speeds.

(i)

Generation of characteristics.

The generation of characteristics emitting from points on the
x
–axis
is completely solved in Theorem
3.8

by providing and proving the criteria for all the cases of
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

(as the set of characteristic generation values of
x_{0}
) defined via the new solution formula,
which includes the case that the point
x_{0}
is not a Lebesgue point of the initial data function
(
cf.
Fig.
2.2
).

(ii)

Spread of characteristics.

The spread of characteristics is established in Theorems
3.12
–
3.13

via introducing and proving the lifespan of characteristics determined by the new solution formula.

(iii)

Termination of characteristics.

Using the upper bound of lifespan and the lifespan of characteristics,
we provide a classification of characteristics by the way of their terminations in Theorem
3.14
,
especially including the way of terminations by the formation of the continuous shock generation points.

(iv)

Divides as well as their locations and speeds.

The necessary and sufficient conditions respectively for the divides as well as their locations and speeds are provided and proved in Proposition
6.4
and Theorem
6.9
,
in which we determine the locations and speeds of divides
by introducing the convex hull over
(-\infty,\infty)

of the primitive function of the initial data function
(see Figs.
2.4
–
2.6
).

3. Dynamic behaviors of shocks:
The formation and development of shocks, the spread of shocks, and the asymptotic behaviors of shocks.

(i)

Generation of shocks on the
x
–axis and formation of shocks.

The generation of shocks emitting from points on the
x
–axis is established
by proving the necessary and sufficient conditions
for the shock generation points on the
x
–axis in Theorems
3.8
–
3.9
,
and the formation of shocks is obtained by introducing and proving the necessary and sufficient conditions
for all the five types of the continuous shock generation points in Theorem
4.1

and for the discontinuous shock generation points in Theorem
5.6
(iii)
associating with Theorem
5.3
(ii).

(ii)

Development of shocks.

The development of shocks is obtained by proving the optimal regularities of shock curves
and entropy solutions near all the five types of continuous shock generation points in Theorem
4.4
,
in which the continuous shock generation points are not necessarily assumed to be isolated
(
cf
. Fig.
2.8
).

(iii)

Spread of shocks.

The spread of shocks is precisely described by both providing the two associated triangle sequences
and proving the left- and right-derivatives and the piecewise smoothness of shock curves in Theorem
5.5
.

(iv)

Asymptotic behaviors of shocks.

The asymptotic behaviors of shocks are established by both providing
all the path-connected branches of the shock sets of entropy solutions in Theorem
6.12

and proving the large-time properties
especially including the limits of speeds and the left- and right-states of shocks
in Lemmas
7.1
–
7.2
and Theorem
7.3
.

Figure 2.8.
The shock curve
x(t)
for the case that
t_{p}^{+}\neq t_{p}^{-}
.
The left/right figure illustrates the case of shocks formatted by the compression of local characteristics
emitting from points on the left/right of
x_{0}
.

4. Influences of the convexity degeneracy of flux functions.

The convexity degeneracy of the flux function
f(u)

(
the infinitesimal order of
f^{\prime\prime}(u)
at degeneracy point
)

influences the behaviors of entropy solutions substantially
via reducing the strength of the compression of characteristics.

These especially include:

(i)

The generation of initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
including the types of characteristics and shocks,
as shown in Propositions
3.6
–
3.7
.

(ii)

The formation and development of shocks,
as shown in Theorems
4.1
and
4.4
.

(iii)

The decay rates respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm,
as shown in Theorems
7.4
–
7.5

and in Theorem
7.9
with Lemma
7.8
, respectively.

Innovation points.

The innovation points on the new solution formula and the fine properties of entropy solutions
can be summarized as follows:

(i)

New solution formula and its derivation.

We first introduce and validate a novel formula for entropy solutions of scalar hyperbolic conservation laws
with flux functions of convexity degeneracy
that allow the state points of convexity degeneracy and asymptotic lines,
which aligns with the well-known Lax–Oleinik formula
[
39
,
40
,
49
]

in the case that the flux function is uniformly convex.

Moreover, the derivation of this new formula is rooted in the understanding of
the intrinsic formula for smooth solutions,
which offers a new approach to identifying formulas
for entropy solutions in more general hyperbolic conservation laws
where such intrinsic formulas are available.

(ii)

Global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions.

The global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions
with
general initial data in
L^{\infty}
are new,
even for the case when the flux function
f(u)
is uniformly convex.
The global dynamic patterns of entropy solutions are established
by leveraging this newly derived formula and introducing new concepts,
which especially include:

(a)

Fine structures of entropy solutions.

By introducing the set
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
of the speeds of characteristic lines
emitting from any
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis, the criteria for all six types of initial waves
for the Cauchy problem are established.
Introducing the set
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
of the speeds of the shock-free backward characteristics
emitting from any point
(x,t)
in the upper half-plane and the lifespan of characteristics,
we obtain the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles,
the directional limits of entropy solutions at discontinuous points,
the left-derivatives of shock curves,
and the formation and development of shocks for all five types of
continuous shock generation points.

(b)

Global structures of entropy solutions.

By introducing the invariants of entropy solutions and the convex hull over
({-}\infty,\infty)
of
the primitive function of the initial data function,
the necessary and sufficient conditions respectively
for the divides as well as their locations and speeds are provided,
and the global structures of entropy solutions are established.

(c)

Asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions.

By introducing the generalized
N
–waves and using the results on invariants and divides developed in this paper,
the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions (including the asymptotic profiles and decay rates)
respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{\rm loc}
–norm are established.

Comparison with the previous results.

The comparison between the previously available results and the results in this paper
can be summarized as follows:
The previously available results, as discussed above,
are mainly restricted to the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)

with the flux function
f(u)
satisfying that

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0\qquad{\rm on}\ u\in\mathbb{R}.

While, in this paper,
the flux function
f(u)
is only required to satisfy (
1.3
),
i.e.
,

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 0\,\,\,\,{\rm on}\ u\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\,\,\,\mathcal{L}\{u\,:\,f^{\prime\prime}(u)=0\}=0,

not only for the results of the new solution formula,
but also for the results on the fine properties of entropy solutions
of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
with

initial data only in
L^{\infty}
.

In §2, since the Lax–Oleinik formula
[
39
,
40
,
49
]
requires that
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
,
it can not be applied directly to
the case of convex functions
f(u)
with
f^{\prime\prime}(u)=0
at some points and/or with asymptotic lines,
such as
f(u)=u^{2n}
with
n>1
and
f(u)=e^{ku}
with
k\neq 0
.
From
(
1.3
)
, our new solution formula is valid for such cases.
Moreover, as shown in Theorem
2.4
,
the entropy solutions of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
also possess strong traces,
even when the flux function
f(u)
in
(
1.3
)
has the state points of convexity degeneracy.

As in §3, for the initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
when
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
,
Lin
[
44
]
showed that there exist six types of
initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
and for the special case that the initial data functions possess
discontinuity points only of the first kind,
and then
gave a sufficient condition of shocks emitting from
continuous points of the initial data function
and a necessary condition of that, respectively;
However, the necessary and sufficient condition to judge
the concrete type of initial waves emitting from
points on the
x
–axis have been opened.
In this paper, for the flux functions satisfying
(
1.3
)

and the initial data only in
L^{\infty}
,
the criteria for all six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
is established by providing the necessary and sufficient conditions
for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem in Theorems
3.8
–
3.9
,
even for the case that the point is not a Lebesgue point of the initial data function.
Furthermore, we introduce and prove the lifespan of characteristics in Theorems
3.12
–
3.13
,
and provide a classification of characteristics by the way of their terminations in Theorem
3.14
.

As in §4, for the formation and development of shocks,
Lebaud
[
41
]
and Yin–Zhu
[
62
]
proved that the singularity
around the first blowup point caused by the initial compression is actually a shock,
under some specific degenerate information of sufficiently smooth initial data functions.
In §4.1, we discover all the five types of continuous shock generation points
and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions of them in Theorem
4.1
;
in §4.2, we prove the optimal regularities of shock curves and entropy solutions
near all the five types of continuous shock generation points in Theorems
4.4
,
in which the continuous shock generation points
are not necessarily assumed to be isolated.

As in §5, for the fine structures of entropy solutions,
when
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
,
many results about the local structures of entropy solutions
by the method of generalized characteristics
are presented in Dafermos
[
22
, Chapter 11]
;
also see Huang
[
35
]
for a description of the
backward characteristic triangles and the shock curves.
However, there exists no results on
the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles
and the directional limits of entropy solutions at the discontinuous points.
Even when the flux function
f(u)
has the state points of convexity degeneracy as in
(
1.3
)
, we provide and prove
the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles
in Theorem
5.3
and
the directional limits of entropy solutions at the discontinuous points
(including the case for the points at which there are infinitely many shocks collided together)
in Theorem
5.4

via the vital exploitation of the new solution formula.

As in §6, for the invariants, divides, and global structures of entropy solutions, when

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
, Proposition
6.4

was given by Dafermos
[
22
, Theorem 11.4.1]
,
and some description of divides (without the locations and speeds)
and the global structures of shocks were given in Li
[
42
]
.
As shown in Theorems
6.9
–
6.10
,
for general initial data in
L^{\infty}
,
the necessary and sufficient conditions respectively for the divides as well as their locations and speeds of entropy solutions are provided and proved.
As an illustration, the divides of entropy solutions for the
L^{\infty}
initial data functions
with compact support, periodicity, or
L^{1}
-integrability, respectively,
are given in Examples
6.1
–
6.3
.
Furthermore, the global structures of entropy solutions with general initial data functions in
L^{\infty}
are obtained in Theorem
6.12
.

As in §7, for the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions,
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq c_{0}>0
,
Lax
[
39
,
40
]
gave the invariants of entropy solutions
for the initial data in
L^{\infty}\cap L^{1}

and obtained the development of
N
–waves for the initial data with compact support
and the formation of sawtooth profiles for the periodic initial data;
Dafermos
[
19
,
22
]
obtained several important
cases of
the asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions by developing the method of generalized characteristics;
and Li–Wang
[
43
]
gave several asymptotic behaviors of entropy solutions
for some types of smooth initial data functions.
For the flux functions satisfying
(
1.3
)

and the initial data only in
L^{\infty}
,
we establish the asymptotic behaviors by proving
the detailed asymptotic profiles and the corresponding decay rates of entropy solutions
respectively in both the
L^{\infty}
–norm in Theorems
7.3
–
7.5

and the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm in Lemma
7.8
and Theorem
7.9
.

2.3.
Proofs of the solution formula and its corollaries

In this subsection, we first prove Theorem
2.4
on the new solution formula,
and then prove Corollaries
2.5
–
2.6
.

Before giving the proofs, we introduce some notions:
For any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote the set:

(2.20)

{\rm Con}(x,t,u):=\big\{(\xi,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\,:\,\tau f^{\prime}({-}\infty)<\xi-y(x,t,u)<\tau f^{\prime}(\infty)\big\},

where
y(x,t,u)
is defined by

(2.21)

y(x,t,u):=x-tf^{\prime}(u).

Then, from
(
2.20
)
,
{\rm Con}(x,t,u)\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
is open
so that there exists
\delta>0
such that
B_{\delta}(x,t)\subset{\rm Con}(x,t,u)
.
In particular,

{\rm Con}(x,t,u)\equiv\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\qquad\,\mbox{ if $f^{\prime}(\pm\infty)=\pm\infty$}.

Since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,
(
2.20
)
–
(
2.21
)
imply that,
for any
(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in{\rm Con}(x,t,u)
,
there exists a unique
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.22)

y(x,t,u)=x-tf^{\prime}(u)=x^{\prime}-t^{\prime}f^{\prime}(\hat{u})=y(x^{\prime},t^{\prime},\hat{u}),

which, by
(
2.20
)
, implies

(2.23)

{\rm Con}(x,t,u)={\rm Con}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime},\hat{u}).

See also Fig.
2.9
.

By
(
2.22
)
, it follows from
(
2.4
)
that

(2.24)

E(\hat{u};x^{\prime},t^{\prime})-E(u;x,t)=\int^{x^{\prime}-t^{\prime}f^{\prime}(0)}_{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-(t^{\prime}-t)\int^{\hat{u}}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t\int^{\hat{u}}_{u}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s.

Figure 2.9.
{\rm Con}(x,t,u)={\rm Con}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime},\hat{u})
as in
(
2.23
)
.

2.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
2.4

From
(
2.4
)
–
(
2.5
)
,

\mathcal{U}(x,t)\subset[-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}]

for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Then it follows from
(
2.6
)
that,
for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(2.25)

|u^{\pm}(x,t)|\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}.

The remaining proof is divided into five steps.

1.
For
\hat{E}(x,t)
defined by
(
2.7
)
, we have

Lemma 2.7
.

\hat{E}(x,t)
as in
(
2.7
)
is Lipschitz continuous
and satisfies the following properties
:

(i)

For any
t>0
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

(2.26)

\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}u^{\pm}(x,t)\,{\rm d}x=\hat{E}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}(x_{2},t).

(ii)

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{2}>t_{1}>0
,

(2.27)

\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}f(u^{\pm}(x,t))\,{\rm d}t=\hat{E}(x,t_{2})-\hat{E}(x,t_{1}).

Proof of
Lemma

2.7
.

We divide the proof into three steps.

(a).
We first show that
\hat{E}(x,t)\in C(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
.
By the definition of
\hat{E}(x,t)
in
(
2.7
)
, it suffices to prove that
E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)\in C(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
.

For any fixed
(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
denote
u^{+}_{0}:=u^{+}(x_{0},t_{0})
.
It follows from
(
2.4
)
that

\displaystyle E(u^{+}_{0};x,t)=-\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}_{0})}_{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int^{u^{+}_{0}}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s,

which means that
E(u^{+}_{0};x,t)
is continuous in
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
Thus, for any given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta_{1}>0
such that,
for any
(x,t)\in B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{0},t_{0})
,

E(u^{+}_{0};x,t)>E(u^{+}_{0};x_{0},t_{0})-\varepsilon,

which, by
u^{+}(x,t)\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
, implies that,
for any
(x,t)\in B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{0},t_{0})
,

(2.28)

\displaystyle E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)\geq E(u^{+}_{0};x,t)>E(u^{+}_{0};x_{0},t_{0})-\varepsilon.

On the other hand, since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,
from
(
2.21
)
,
for any
(x,t)\in B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{0},t_{0})
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle\big|y(x,t,u^{+}(x,t))-y(x_{0},t_{0},u^{+}(x,t))\big|<N\delta_{1},\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle y(x_{0},t_{0},M)\leq y(x_{0},t_{0},u^{+}(x,t))\leq y(x_{0},t_{0},-M),\end{cases}

where
N:=\sup_{|u|\leq M}\sqrt{1+f^{\prime}(u)^{2}}

for
M:=\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Then
\delta_{1}
can be further chosen sufficiently small,
if needed,
such that

y(x_{0},t_{0},2M)\leq y(x,t,u^{+}(x,t))\leq y(x_{0},t_{0},-2M)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ any\ }(x,t)\in B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{0},t_{0}).

This implies that,
for any
(x,t)\in B_{\delta_{1}}(x_{0},t_{0})
, there exists a unique
\hat{u}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.29)

x-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t))=x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(\hat{u}).

Since
\varphi(x)
and
u^{+}(x,t)
are both bounded,
it follows from
(
2.24
)
,
(
2.29
)
, and
(
A.2
)
that

\displaystyle\ E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)-E(\hat{u};x_{0},t_{0})

\displaystyle=\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-(t-t_{0})\int^{u^{+}(x,t)}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t_{0}\int_{\hat{u}}^{u^{+}(x,t)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-(t-t_{0})\int^{u^{+}(x,t)}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t_{0}\,\rho(u^{+}(x,t),\hat{u})\big(f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t))-f^{\prime}(\hat{u})\big)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow 0\qquad\,\,\,{\rm as}\ (x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},t_{0}).

This implies that, for sufficiently small
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
,
if
(x,t)\in B_{\delta}(x_{0},t_{0})
, then

(2.30)

\displaystyle E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)<E(\hat{u};x_{0},t_{0})+\varepsilon\leq E(u^{+}_{0};x_{0},t_{0})+\varepsilon.

Combining
(
2.28
)
with
(
2.30
)
yields that,
for any given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that

|E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)-E(u^{+}_{0};x_{0},t_{0})|<\varepsilon\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ (x,t)\in B_{\delta}(x_{0},t_{0}).

By the arbitrariness of
(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
we conclude that

E(u^{+}(x,t);x,t)\in C(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}),

which, by
(
2.7
)
, implies that
\hat{E}(x,t)\in C(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
.

(b).
We now prove
(
2.26
)
for the case of
u^{+}(x,t)
only, since
the case of
u^{-}(x,t)
is similar.

For any
t>0
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
u^{+}_{i}:=u^{+}(x_{i},t)
for
i=1,2
.
By the additivity of
(
2.26
)
,
it suffices to prove
(
2.26
)
for small
|x_{2}-x_{1}|
.

Since
|u^{+}_{i}|\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
it follows from
(
2.20
)
–
(
2.21
)
that,
for small
|x_{2}-x_{1}|
,

(x_{1},t)\in{\rm Con}(x_{2},t,u^{+}_{2}),\qquad(x_{2},t)\in{\rm Con}(x_{1},t,u^{+}_{1}),

so that, by
(
2.22
)
,
there exists a unique
(\hat{u}_{1},\hat{u}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

(2.31)

x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(\hat{u}_{1})=x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2}),\qquad x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})=x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(\hat{u}_{2}).

Since
u^{+}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(x_{i},t)
,
by
(
2.7
)
and
(
2.31
)
,
it follows from
(
2.24
)
and
(
A.2
)
that

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}(x_{2},t)

\displaystyle\geq E(\hat{u}_{1};x_{1},t)-E(u^{+}_{2};x_{2},t)-\int^{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=t\int^{u^{+}_{2}}_{\hat{u}_{1}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=(u^{+}_{2}+o(1))t\big(f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2})-f^{\prime}(\hat{u}_{1})\big)

(2.32)

\displaystyle=-(u^{+}_{2}+o(1))(x_{1}-x_{2}),

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}(x_{2},t)

\displaystyle\leq E(u^{+}_{1};x_{1},t)-E(\hat{u}_{2};x_{2},t)-\int^{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=t\int_{u^{+}_{1}}^{\hat{u}_{2}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=(u^{+}_{1}+o(1))t\big(f^{\prime}(\hat{u}_{2})-f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})\big)

(2.33)

\displaystyle=-(u^{+}_{1}+o(1))(x_{1}-x_{2}).

By the arbitrariness of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
,
it follows from
(
2.32
)
–
(
2.33
)
that
(
2.26
)
holds.

(c).
We now prove
(
2.27
)
for
u^{+}(x,t)
only,
since the case for
u^{-}(x,t)
is similar.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{2}>t_{1}>0
, denote
u^{+}_{i}:=u^{+}(x,t_{i})
for
i=1,2
.
By the additivity of
(
2.27
)
,
it suffices to prove
(
2.27
)
for small
t_{2}-t_{1}>0
.

Since
|u^{+}_{i}|\leq\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
it follows from
(
2.20
)
–
(
2.21
)
that,
for small
t_{2}-t_{1}>0
,

(x,t_{1})\in{\rm Con}(x,t_{2},u^{+}_{2}),\qquad(x,t_{2})\in{\rm Con}(x,t_{1},u^{+}_{1}),

so that, by
(
2.22
)
,
there exists a unique
(\check{u}_{1},\check{u}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

(2.34)

x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(\check{u}_{1})=x-t_{2}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2}),\qquad x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})=x-t_{2}f^{\prime}(\check{u}_{2}).

Since
u^{+}_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(x_{i},t)
,
from
(
2.7
)
and
(
2.34
)
,
it follows from
(
2.24
)
and
(
A.2
)
that

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,t_{1})-\hat{E}(x,t_{2})

\displaystyle\geq E(\check{u}_{1};x,t_{1})-E(u^{+}_{2};x,t_{2})-\int^{x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(0)}_{x-t_{2}f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=(t_{2}-t_{1})\int^{u^{+}_{2}}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+t_{1}\int^{u^{+}_{2}}_{\check{u}_{1}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\big(u^{+}_{2}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2})-f(u^{+}_{2})\big)(t_{2}-t_{1})+(u^{+}_{2}+o(1))t_{1}\big(f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2})-f^{\prime}(\check{u}_{1})\big)

\displaystyle=-\big(u^{+}_{2}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2})-f(u^{+}_{2})\big)(t_{1}-t_{2})+(u^{+}_{2}+o(1))f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{2})(t_{1}-t_{2})

(2.35)

\displaystyle=\big(f(u^{+}_{2})+o(1)\big)(t_{1}-t_{2}),

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,t_{1})-\hat{E}(x,t_{2})

\displaystyle\leq E(u^{+}_{1};x,t_{1})-E(\check{u}_{2};x,t_{2})-\int^{x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(0)}_{x-t_{2}f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=(t_{2}-t_{1})\int^{u^{+}_{1}}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t_{2}\int^{u^{+}_{1}}_{\check{u}_{2}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\big(u^{+}_{1}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})-f(u^{+}_{1})\big)(t_{2}-t_{1})-(u^{+}_{1}+o(1))t_{2}\big(f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})-f^{\prime}(\check{u}_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=-\big(u^{+}_{1}f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})-f(u^{+}_{1})\big)(t_{1}-t_{2})+(u^{+}_{1}+o(1))f^{\prime}(u^{+}_{1})(t_{1}-t_{2})

(2.36)

\displaystyle=\big(f(u^{+}_{1})+o(1)\big)(t_{1}-t_{2}).

By the arbitrariness of
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
,
it follows from
(
2.35
)
–
(
2.36
)
that
(
2.27
)
holds.
∎

2.
We now prove the Oleinik-type one-sided inequality
(
2.3
)
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any
t>0
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
x<x^{\prime}

(
see
Fig.

\ref{figOleinik})
,

(2.37)

x-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t))\leq x^{\prime}-tf^{\prime}(u^{-}(x^{\prime},t)).

Furthermore,
y(x,t,u^{\pm}(x,t))
defined by
(
2.21
)
are both nondecreasing in
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Figure 2.10.
The Oleinik-type one-sided inequality as in
(
2.37
)
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.8
.

From
(
2.6
)
,
u^{+}(x,t)\leq u^{-}(x,t)
, which implies

x-tf^{\prime}(u^{-}(x,t))\leq x-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t)),\qquad x^{\prime}-tf^{\prime}(u^{-}(x^{\prime},t))\leq x^{\prime}-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}(x^{\prime},t)).

Then it suffices to prove
(
2.37
)
for small
x^{\prime}-x>0
.

Fix
t>0
. For any
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
x<x^{\prime}
such that
(x^{\prime},t)\in{\rm Con}(x,t,u^{+}(x,t))
,
denote
\delta:=x^{\prime}-x>0
and
u^{+}:=u^{+}(x,t)
.
Since
(x^{\prime},t)\in{\rm Con}(x,t,u^{+}(x,t))
, from
(
2.22
)
,
there exists a unique
\hat{u}^{+}
such that
y(x^{\prime},t,\hat{u}^{+})=y(x,t,u^{+})
,
i.e.
,

(2.38)

x^{\prime}-tf^{\prime}(\hat{u}^{+})=x-tf^{\prime}(u^{+}).

Then, for any
u>\hat{u}^{+}
,

y(x^{\prime},t,u)<y(x^{\prime},t,\hat{u}^{+})=y(x,t,u^{+}).

Since
x<x^{\prime}
, then

x-y(x,t,u^{+})<x-y(x^{\prime},t,u)<x^{\prime}-y(x^{\prime},t,u),

which, by
(
2.20
)
, implies that
(x,t)\in{\rm Con}(x^{\prime},t,u)
for any
u>\hat{u}^{+}
.
This, by
(
2.22
)
, infers that there exists a unique
\hat{u}
such that

(2.39)

x^{\prime}-tf^{\prime}(u)=x-tf^{\prime}(\hat{u}).

Since
u^{+}\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
,
then
E(\hat{u};x,t)-E(u^{+};x,t)\leq 0.

By
(
2.24
)
and
(
2.38
)
–
(
2.39
)
, for any
u>\hat{u}^{+}
,

\displaystyle\ E(u;x^{\prime},t)-E(\hat{u}^{+};x^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle=\big(E(u;x^{\prime},t)-E(\hat{u};x,t)\big)+\big(E(\hat{u};x,t)-E(u^{+};x,t)\big)+\big(E(u^{+};x,t)-E(\hat{u}^{+};x^{\prime},t)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big(E(u;x^{\prime},t)-E(\hat{u};x,t)\big)+\big(E(u^{+};x,t)-E(\hat{u}^{+};x^{\prime},t)\big)

\displaystyle=-t\int^{u}_{\hat{u}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+t\int^{\hat{u}^{+}}_{u^{+}}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

(2.40)

\displaystyle=:-t\big(L(u)-L(\hat{u}^{+})\big).

It follows from
(
2.39
)
that

u>\hat{u}
and
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\,{\rm d}u=f^{\prime\prime}(\hat{u})\,{\rm d}\hat{u}
so that

\displaystyle{\rm d}L(u)=uf^{\prime\prime}(u)\,{\rm d}u-\hat{u}f^{\prime\prime}(\hat{u})\,{\rm d}\hat{u}=(u-\hat{u})f^{\prime\prime}(u)\,{\rm d}u,

which implies that
L^{\prime}(u)\geq 0
.

From
(
1.3
)
,
f^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 0
with
\mathcal{L}\{u\,:\,f^{\prime\prime}(u)=0\}=0
.
Then
\mathcal{L}\{u\,:\,L^{\prime}(u)=0\}=0
so that

L(u)-L(\hat{u}^{+})=\int^{u}_{\hat{u}^{+}}L^{\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s>0\qquad\,\,\mbox{for $u>\hat{u}^{+}$},

which, by
(
2.40
)
, implies that, for any
u>\hat{u}^{+}
,

E(u;x^{\prime},t)-E(\hat{u}^{+};x^{\prime},t)\leq-t\big(L(u)-L(\hat{u}^{+})\big)<0.

This means that
E(\cdot\,;x^{\prime},t)
attains its maximum only if
u\leq\hat{u}^{+}
.
Then it follows from
(
2.5
)
–
(
2.6
)
that
u^{-}(x^{\prime},t)\leq\hat{u}^{+}
,
which, by
(
2.38
)
with
x<x^{\prime}
, yields
(
2.37
)
.
∎

3.
We now prove the properties of
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
in Lemma
2.9
below,
which include the traces of function
u(x,t)=u^{+}(x,t)
and
the one-to-one correspondence between the elements of
\mathcal{U}(x,t)

and the shock-free backward characteristics passing point
(x,t)
in the upper half-space.

Lemma 2.9
.

For any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
, the following statements hold
:

(i)

For any sequence

(x_{n},t_{n})\to(x,t)
as
n\to\infty
, if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u^{\pm}(x_{n},t_{n})
exists, then

(2.41)

\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}u^{\pm}(x_{n},t_{n})\in\mathcal{U}(x,t).

(ii)

For
u^{\pm}(x{-},t):=\lim_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)

and
u^{\pm}(x{+},t):=\lim_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)
,

(2.42)

u^{\pm}(x{-},t)=u^{-}(x,t),\qquad u^{\pm}(x{+},t)=u^{+}(x,t).

(iii)

Fix
(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
.
For any
c\in\mathbb{R}
, define

(2.43)

l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0}):=\big\{(x,t)\,:\,x=x_{0}+(t-t_{0})f^{\prime}(c)\quad{\rm for}\ t\in(0,t_{0})\big\}.

Then

(2.44)

c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{0},t_{0})\qquad{\rm if\ and\ only\ if}\qquad l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})\,\,\mbox{is\ a\ shock-free\ characteristic}.

Proof of
Lemma

2.9
.

We divide the proof into three steps.

(a)
.
Denote
u^{*}:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u^{+}(x_{n},t_{n})
.
Then it suffices to prove that
u^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
.
By Lemma
2.7
,
\hat{E}(x,t)\in C(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
so that

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,t)=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\hat{E}(x_{n},t_{n})

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big(E(u^{+}(x_{n},t_{n});x_{n},t_{n})-\int^{x_{n}-t_{n}f^{\prime}(0)}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi\Big)

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}\Big({-}\int^{x_{n}-t_{n}f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x_{n},t_{n}))}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t_{n}\int^{u^{+}(x_{n},t_{n})}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s\Big)

\displaystyle=-\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(u^{*})}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int^{u^{*}}_{0}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=E(u^{*};x,t)-\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi.

This, by
(
2.7
)
, implies that
E(u^{*};x,t)=\max_{v\in\mathbb{R}}E(v;x,t)

so that
u^{*}\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
.
Similarly, it is direct to check that
(
2.41
)
holds for
u^{-}(x,t)
.

(b).
From
(
2.6
)
and
(
2.41
)
,

(2.45)

u^{+}(x,t)\leq\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rightarrow(x,t)}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\leq\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\rightarrow(x,t)}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})\leq u^{-}(x,t).

When
x^{\prime}<x
, from Lemma
2.8
,

f^{\prime}(u^{-}(x,t))+\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{t}\leq f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x^{\prime},t))\leq f^{\prime}(u^{-}(x^{\prime},t)).

As
x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}
, it follows from
(
2.45
)
that

f^{\prime}(u^{-}(x,t))\leq\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}}f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t))\leq\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}}f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t))\leq f^{\prime}(u^{-}(x,t)),

which, by the strictly increasing property of
f^{\prime}(u)
, implies

u^{-}(x,t)\leq\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)\leq\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{-}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)\leq u^{-}(x,t).

This means that
u^{\pm}(x{-},t)=u^{-}(x,t)
.

When
x^{\prime}>x
, from Lemma
2.8
,

f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t))+\frac{x^{\prime}-x}{t}\geq f^{\prime}(u^{-}(x^{\prime},t))\geq f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x^{\prime},t)).

As
x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}
, it follows from
(
2.45
)
that

f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t))\geq\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}}f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t))\geq\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}}f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t))\geq f^{\prime}(u^{+}(x,t)),

which, by the strictly increasing property of
f^{\prime}(u)
, implies

u^{+}(x,t)\geq\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)\geq\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{x^{\prime}\rightarrow x{+}}u^{\pm}(x^{\prime},t)\geq u^{+}(x,t).

This means that
u^{\pm}(x{+},t)=u^{+}(x,t).

(c).
If
c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{0},t_{0})
, then,
for any
(x,t)\in l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})
,

(2.46)

x-tf^{\prime}(c)=x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(c),

so that, for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{x_{0}-x}{t_{0}}+\frac{t}{t_{0}}f^{\prime}(u)=\frac{t_{0}-t}{t_{0}}f^{\prime}(c)+\frac{t}{t_{0}}f^{\prime}(u)

lies between
f^{\prime}(c)
and
f^{\prime}(u)
.
This implies that, for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,
there exists a unique
\hat{u}
between
c
and
u
such that

(2.47)

x-tf^{\prime}(u)=x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(\hat{u}).

Then it follows from
(
2.4
)
and
c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{0},t_{0})
that,
if
u\neq c
,

\displaystyle E(u;x,t)-E(c;x,t)

\displaystyle=-\int^{x-tf^{\prime}(u)}_{x-tf^{\prime}(c)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int^{u}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=-\int^{x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(\hat{u})}_{x_{0}-t_{0}f^{\prime}(c)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int^{u}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=E(\hat{u};x_{0},t_{0})-E(c;x_{0},t_{0})+t_{0}\int^{\hat{u}}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t\int^{u}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

(2.48)

\displaystyle\leq t_{0}\int^{\hat{u}}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t\int^{u}_{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=-l\big(\rho(\hat{u},c)-\rho(u,c)\big),

where
\rho(u,v)
is given by
(
A.1
)
, and

l
is determined by
(
2.46
)
–
(
2.47
)
as

l:=t\big(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u)\big)=t_{0}\big(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(\hat{u})\big).

From
(
A.3
)
,
\rho(\cdot,c)
is strictly increasing.
Since
\hat{u}
lies between
c
and
u
, it follows from
(
2.48
)
that

E(u;x,t)-E(c;x,t)<0
for any
u\neq c
.
Thus,
c
is the unique maximum point of
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
. Therefore,

u^{\pm}(x,t)=c
for any
(x,t)\in l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})
,
which implies that
l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})
is shock-free.

On the other hand, if
u^{\pm}(x,t)=c
for any
(x,t)\in l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})
,
it follows from
(
2.41
)
that

c
,
as the limit of
u^{+}(x,t)
as
(x,t)\rightarrow(x_{0},t_{0})
along
l_{c}(x_{0},t_{0})
,
belongs to
\mathcal{U}(x_{0},t_{0})
.
∎

4.
We now show that
u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
defined by
(
2.8
)
solves
the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
in the distributional sense.

(a).
We first prove
(
2.9
)
–
(
2.10
)
.
From
(
2.8
)
and
(
2.42
)
,
u(x\pm,t)=u^{\pm}(x,t)
pointwise.
From
(
2.45
)
,
u(x-,t)
and
u(x+,t)
are upper- and low-semicontinuous, respectively,
so that
u(x-,t)
and
u(x+,t)
are both continuous at point
(x,t)
if
u^{-}(x,t)=u^{+}(x,t)
.
By Lemma
2.8
,
y(\cdot,t,u^{\pm}(\cdot,t))
is nondecreasing
so that it possesses at most countably discontinuous points for each
t>0
.
Since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing, for each
t>0
,
u^{-}(x,t)=u^{+}(x,t)
also holds
except at most countable points
(x,t)
at which
u^{-}(x,t)>u^{+}(x,t)
.
This yields
(
2.9
)
.

Furthermore, from
(
2.21
)
,

f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(x,t))=\frac{x}{t}-\frac{y(x,t,u^{\pm}(x,t))}{t},

so that, by Lemma
2.8
,

f^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(\cdot,t))
can be expressed by the difference of two nondecreasing functions.
This yields
(
2.10
)
.

(b).
We now prove that
u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})

defined by
(
2.8
)
is a weak solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)

in the sense of Definition
2.1
.
From
(
2.9
)
and Lemma
2.7
,
\hat{E}(x,t)
is Lipschitz continuous and satisfies

\hat{E}_{x}(x,t)\mathop{=}\limits^{a.e.}-u(x,t),\quad\hat{E}_{t}(x,t)\mathop{=}\limits^{a.e.}f(u(x,t))\qquad{\rm on}\ \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Then
(
2.1
)
follows since, for any test function
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\big(u\phi_{t}+f(u)\phi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=\iint_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}}\big({-}\hat{E}_{x}\phi_{t}+\hat{E}_{t}\phi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t=0.

Furthermore, since
u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
,
it follows from
(
2.26
)
that, for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow 0{+}}\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}u(x,t)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0{+}}\big(\hat{E}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}(x_{2},t)\big)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0{+}}\bigg(t\int^{u(x_{1},t)}_{u(x_{2},t)}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\,{\rm d}s+\int^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi\bigg)

\displaystyle=\int^{x_{2}}_{x_{1}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi.

5.
We now prove the uniqueness of the entropy solutions given by the solution formula
(
2.8
)

in the sense of Definition
2.2

(
cf.
Hoff
[
29
]
).

(a)
. Since the flux function
f(u)
satisfies
(
1.3
)
,
consider the first-order and second-order differences of
f(u)
:
For any three different
u,v
, and
w
,

\displaystyle f[u,v]:=\frac{f(u)-f(v)}{u-v}=\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime}(\theta u+(1-\theta)v)\,{\rm d}\theta,

\displaystyle f[u,v,w]:=\dfrac{f[u,v]-f[v,w]}{u-w}=\int^{1}_{0}\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime\prime}(\xi\theta u+(1-\theta)v+(1-\xi)\theta w)\theta\,{\rm d}\xi{\rm d}\theta\geq 0.

Then
f[u,v]
can be extended to a continuously differentiable
function in
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
and
f[u,v,w]
can be extended to a continuous function in
(u,v,w)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

(b)
. Let
u_{1}(x,t),u_{2}(x,t)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})

be two different weak solutions of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)

with the same initial data function
\varphi(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

and satisfy the Oleinik-type one-sided inequality
(
2.3
)
.
Then, for any fixed
t_{1},t_{2}
with
t_{2}>t_{1}>0
,
if
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}\times[t_{1},t_{2}])
,

(2.49)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{i}(x,\cdot)\phi(x,\cdot)\big|^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\,{\rm d}x=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(u_{i}\phi_{t}+f(u_{i})\phi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t\qquad\mbox{for $i=1,2$}.

Meanwhile,
it follows from
(
2.3
)
that, for
i=1,2
,

(2.50)

\partial_{x}f^{\prime}(u_{i}(x,t))\leq\frac{1}{t}\qquad\mbox{in the distributional sense}.

Denote
w=w(x,t):=u_{1}(x,t)-u_{2}(x,t)
. From
(
2.49
)
,

(2.51)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,\cdot)\phi(x,\cdot)\big|^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\,{\rm d}x=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\big(\phi_{t}+f[u_{1},u_{2}]\phi_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t.

(c)
. Now fix
\psi(x)\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R})
, and set

\psi_{1}(x):=\frac{\psi(x)}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int^{x}_{-\infty}|\psi^{\prime}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}\xi,\qquad\psi_{2}(x):=\frac{\psi(x)}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int^{x}_{-\infty}|\psi^{\prime}(\xi)|\,{\rm d}\xi.

Then we have

\psi(x)=\psi_{1}(x)+\psi_{2}(x),\qquad\psi_{1}^{\prime}(x)\geq 0\geq\psi_{2}^{\prime}(x).

For any given
\varepsilon,\delta>0
,
let
\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta},i=1,2,
solve the following linear transport equation respectively:

(2.52)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\partial_{t}}v+\big(j_{\varepsilon}\ast f^{\prime}(u_{i})\big)\,\partial_{x}v=0,\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle v(x,t_{2})=(j_{\delta}\ast\psi_{i})(x),\end{cases}

where
j_{\varepsilon},j_{\delta}>0
are the modifiers satisfying that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}j_{\varepsilon}(x)\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}j_{\delta}(x)\,{\rm d}x=1

for
\varepsilon,\delta>0
.

Since
\psi
is bounded and
{\rm spt}(\psi)
is a bounded set,
then
\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}\times[t_{1},t_{2}])
.
Taking the derivative
\partial_{x}
on
(
2.52
)
,
we obtain that
\partial_{x}\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
solves the following linear equation of
v_{x}
:

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}(v_{x})+\big(j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{i})\big)\,\partial_{x}(v_{x})=-\big(j_{\varepsilon}*\partial_{x}(f^{\prime}(u_{i}))\big)\,v_{x},\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle v_{x}(x,t_{2})=(j_{\delta}*\psi^{\prime}_{i})(x).\end{cases}

Then, along any characteristic
x(t):\,\frac{{\rm d}x(t)}{{\rm d}t}=(j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{i}))(x(t),t)

with
x(t_{2})=y
,

\,\partial_{x}\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
satisfies

\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{i}(x(t),t)=(j_{\delta}*\psi^{\prime}_{i})(y)\exp\Big\{\int^{t_{2}}_{t}\big(j_{\varepsilon}*\partial_{x}(f^{\prime}(u_{i}))\big)(x(\tau),\tau)\,{\rm d}\tau\Big\},

and hence
\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\geq 0\geq\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}
.
Thus, it follows from
(
2.50
)
that

(2.53)

\big|\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{i}(x,t)\big|\leq\|\psi^{\prime}_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}\exp\Big\{\int^{t_{2}}_{t}\frac{1}{\tau}\,{\rm d}\tau\Big\}=\frac{t_{2}}{t}\|\psi^{\prime}_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

(d)
. Let
\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}:=\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}
.
Then
\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}\in C^{\infty}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}\times[t_{1},t_{2}])
.
Taking
\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}
into
(
2.51
)
–
(
2.52
)
,

\displaystyle\ \int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{2})(j_{\delta}*\psi)(x)\,{\rm d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{1})\,\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}(x,t_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\big((\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2})_{t}+f[u_{1},u_{2}](\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2})_{x}\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

\displaystyle=\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f[u_{1},u_{2}]-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\quad\,+\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}\,\big(f[u_{1},u_{2}]-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{2})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(2.54)

\displaystyle=:A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}.

For
A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}
,
since
f[u_{1},u_{1}]=f^{\prime}(u_{1})
and
f[u_{1},u_{1},u_{2}]\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\ w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f[u_{1},u_{2}]-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big)

\displaystyle=w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f[u_{1},u_{2}]-f[u_{1},u_{1}]\big)+w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big)

\displaystyle=-w^{2}\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,f[u_{1},u_{1},u_{2}]+w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big)

\displaystyle\leq w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big).

Since both functions
\psi_{i}(x),i=1,2
, are compact supported,
then there exists sufficiently large
R>0
such that

{\rm spt}(\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta})\subset[-R,R]\times[0,t_{2}]=:\mathbb{D}

for any
\varepsilon,\delta>0
and
i=1,2
.
Thus, from
(
2.53
)
,

\displaystyle A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\int^{R}_{-R}w\,\partial_{x}\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\,\big(f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big)\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int^{t_{2}}_{t_{1}}\,\int^{R}_{-R}\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\frac{t_{2}}{t}\|\psi^{\prime}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\big|f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\big|\,{\rm d}x{\rm d}t

(2.55)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\,\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|\psi^{\prime}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}.

Similarly, we have

(2.56)

\displaystyle A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}\leq\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\,\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|\psi^{\prime}_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|f^{\prime}(u_{2})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{2})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}.

From
(
2.52
)
,
\|\phi_{i}^{\varepsilon,\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\psi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Combining
(
2.54
)
with
(
2.55
)
–
(
2.56
)
leads to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{2})\,(j_{\delta}*\psi)(x)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{1})\,\phi^{\varepsilon,\delta}(x,t_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}+A^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{2}+\|w(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}\,\big(\|\psi_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\psi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\,\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|\psi^{\prime}_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|f^{\prime}(u_{1})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\frac{t_{2}}{t_{1}}\,\|w\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|\psi^{\prime}_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|f^{\prime}(u_{2})-j_{\varepsilon}*f^{\prime}(u_{2})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\|w(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}\,\big(\|\psi_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\psi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big).

This, by letting
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0{+}
, implies

\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{2})\,(j_{\delta}\ast\psi)(x)\,{\rm d}x\leq\|w(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{D})}\,\big(\|\psi_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\psi_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}}\big).

Letting
t_{1}\rightarrow 0{+}
first and then

\delta\rightarrow 0{+}
, we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}}w(x,t_{2})\,\psi(x)\,{\rm d}x\leq 0\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ \psi(x)\in C_{\rm c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}).

This means that, for any
t_{2}>0
,
w(x,t_{2})=0
holds almost everywhere for
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
By the arbitrariness of
t_{2}>0
and the Fubini theorem,

w(x,t)=0
holds almost everywhere for
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
i.e.
,

u_{1}(x,t)=u_{2}(x,t)
holds almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

After all the five steps above, we complete the proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Remark 2.4
.

For any
x\neq x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}>t
,

(2.57)

\frac{U(x,t^{\prime},x^{\prime})-U(x,t,x^{\prime})}{t^{\prime}-t}+\frac{F(x^{\prime},t,t^{\prime})-F(x,t,t^{\prime})}{x^{\prime}-x}=0,

where
U(x,t,x^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{x^{\prime}-x}\int^{x^{\prime}}_{x}u(\xi,t)\,{\rm d}\xi
and

F(x,t,t^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t}\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}f(u(x,\tau))\,{\rm d}\tau.

In fact, this can be seen as follows:
By Lemma
2.7
, for any
x\neq x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}>t
,

\displaystyle\int^{x^{\prime}}_{x}u(\xi,t)\,{\rm d}\xi=\hat{E}(x,t)-\hat{E}(x^{\prime},t),

\displaystyle\int^{x^{\prime}}_{x}u(\xi,t^{\prime})\,{\rm d}\xi=\hat{E}(x,t^{\prime})-\hat{E}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}f(u(x,\tau))\,{\rm d}\tau=\hat{E}(x,t^{\prime})-\hat{E}(x,t),

\displaystyle\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}f(u(x^{\prime},\tau))\,{\rm d}\tau=\hat{E}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})-\hat{E}(x^{\prime},t),

so that

\displaystyle\int^{x^{\prime}}_{x}\big(u(\xi,t^{\prime})-u(\xi,t)\big)\,{\rm d}\xi+\int^{t^{\prime}}_{t}\big(f(u(x^{\prime},\tau))-f(u(x,\tau))\big)\,{\rm d}\tau=0,

which, by multiplying
\frac{1}{(x^{\prime}-x)(t^{\prime}-t)}
, implies
(
2.57
)
.

2.3.2.
Proof of Corollary
2.5

The proof is divided into three steps.

1.
We first prove the
L^{1}-
contraction inequality
(
2.11
)
.
Let the entropy solutions
u_{i}(x,t)
be determined by
E_{i}(u;x,t)
as in
(
2.4
)

with initial data functions
\varphi_{i}(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
for
i=1,2
.

(a).
We claim: For any
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
,

(2.58)

\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1,1})}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2,2})}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x\leq\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2,1})}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1,2})}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x,

where
u_{i,j}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}(x_{j},t)
for
i,j=1,2
.

In fact, for any fixed
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
,

u_{2,1}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(x_{1},t)
and
u_{1,2}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}(x_{2},t)
.
From
(
2.7
)
,
taking
u_{i}(x,t)
into
(
2.26
)
respectively and subtracting them yield

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\big(\hat{E}_{2}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}_{1}(x_{1},t)\big)-\big(\hat{E}_{2}(x_{2},t)-\hat{E}_{1}(x_{2},t)\big)

\displaystyle\leq\big(E_{2}(u_{2,1};x_{1},t)-E_{1}(u_{2,1};x_{1},t)\big)-\big(E_{2}(u_{1,2};x_{2},t)-E_{1}(u_{1,2};x_{2},t)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,\,+\int^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\big(\varphi_{2}(\xi)-\varphi_{1}(\xi)\big)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=t\int_{0}^{u_{2,1}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad\,-t\int_{0}^{u_{1,2}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\quad\,+\int^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\big(\varphi_{2}(\xi)-\varphi_{1}(\xi)\big)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\bigg({-}\int^{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2,1})}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}{+}\int_{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1,2})}{+}\int^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\bigg)\big(\varphi_{2}(\xi)-\varphi_{1}(\xi)\big)\,{\rm d}\xi

(2.59)

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2,1})}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1,2})}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x.

Similarly, from
u_{1,1}\in\mathcal{U}_{1}(x_{1},t)
and
u_{2,2}\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(x_{2},t)
,
it is direct to check that

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\big(\hat{E}_{2}(x_{1},t)-\hat{E}_{1}(x_{1},t)\big)-\big(\hat{E}_{2}(x_{2},t)-\hat{E}_{1}(x_{2},t)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\big(E_{2}(u_{1,1};x_{1},t)-E_{1}(u_{1,1};x_{1},t)\big)-\big(E_{2}(u_{2,2};x_{2},t)-E_{1}(u_{2,2};x_{2},t)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,\,+\int^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(0)}_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(0)}\big(\varphi_{2}(\xi)-\varphi_{1}(\xi)\big)\,{\rm d}\xi

(2.60)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1,1})}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2,2})}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x.

Combining
(
2.59
)
with
(
2.60
)
, we obtain
(
2.58
)
.

(b).
From
(
2.21
)
, for any
t>0
,

f^{\prime}(u_{2}(x\pm,t))-f^{\prime}(u_{1}(x\pm,t))=\frac{y_{1}(x,t,u_{1}(x\pm,t))-y_{2}(x,t,u_{2}(x\pm,t))}{t}.

Let
y^{\pm}_{i}(x,t):=y_{i}(x,t,u_{i}(x\pm,t))
for
i=1,2
.
Since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,
f^{\prime}(u_{2}(x\pm,t))-f^{\prime}(u_{1}(x\pm,t))
and hence

y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)-y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)
have the same signs
as
u_{2}(x\pm,t)-u_{1}(x\pm,t)
.

Since
y^{\pm}_{1}(\cdot,t)
and
y^{\pm}_{2}(\cdot,t)
are both nondecreasing in
x
,
then, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a<b
, the set:

\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)<a\big\}\cap\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)>b\big\}

is the intersection of two half-lines.
By removing at most countable points at which
y^{\pm}_{1}(\cdot,t)
or
y^{\pm}_{2}(\cdot,t)
is discontinuous,
we see that

\big\{x\,:\,y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)-y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)<0\big\}={\bigcup}_{a<b;\,a,b\in\mathbb{Q}}\big(\big\{x\,:\,y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)<a\big\}\cap\big\{x\,:\,y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)>b\big\}\big)

is an open set, so it is a countable union of disjoint open intervals

\cup_{n}X_{n}:=\cup_{n}(x_{n,l},x_{n,r})
.
Similarly, the set:
\{x\,:\,y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)-y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)>0\}

is a countable union of disjoint open intervals

\cup_{m}Y_{m}:=\cup_{m}(y_{m,l},y_{m,r})
.
Furthermore, any two open intervals in
\{X_{n},Y_{m}\}_{n,m}
are disjoint.

For
x_{1}<x_{2}
, denote
[x_{1}(0),\,x_{2}(0)]:=[x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(M(x_{1},t)),\,x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(m(x_{2},t))]
.
Furthermore, for
X_{n},Y_{m}
in
\{X_{n},Y_{m}\}_{n,m}
, denote

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{n}(0):=(x_{n,l}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1}(x_{n,l},t)),\,x_{n,r}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2}(x_{n,r},t))),\\[5.69054pt]
\displaystyle Y_{m}(0):=(y_{m,l}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2}(y_{m,l},t)),\,y_{m,r}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1}(y_{m,r},t))).\end{cases}

From
(
2.58
)
, for any
t>0
and
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
with
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
