\displaystyle\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap\{u_{2}-u_{1}>0\}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap\{y^{\pm}_{1}(x,t)-y^{\pm}_{2}(x,t)>0\}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap(y_{m,l},y_{m,r})}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

(2.61)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap Y_{m}(0)}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x,

where the last step is inferred by
(
2.58
)
with the following facts:
For the case that
x_{1}\in(y_{m,l},y_{m,r})
for some
m
,
x_{1}(0)=x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(u_{2}(x_{1},t))
;
for the case that
x_{2}\in(y_{m^{\prime},l},y_{m^{\prime},r})
for some
m^{\prime}
,
x_{2}(0)=x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(u_{1}(x_{2},t))
.
Similarly, we have

(2.62)

\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap\{u_{2}-u_{1}<0\}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x\geq\sum_{n}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap X_{n}(0)}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x.

Since any two open intervals in
\{X_{n},Y_{m}\}_{n,m}
are disjoint,
by
(
2.37
)
,
any two open intervals in
\{X_{n}(0),Y_{m}(0)\}_{n,m}
do not intersect each other.
From
(
2.61
)
–
(
2.62
)
, we conclude

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}}^{x_{2}}|u_{2}-u_{1}|\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap\{u_{2}-u_{1}<0\}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x+\int_{[x_{1},x_{2}]\bigcap\{u_{2}-u_{1}>0\}}(u_{2}-u_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq-\sum_{n}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap X_{n}(0)}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x+\sum_{m}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap Y_{m}(0)}(\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{n}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap X_{n}(0)}+\sum_{m}\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]\bigcap Y_{m}(0)}\bigg)|\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1}|\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[x_{1}(0),x_{2}(0)]}|\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1}|\,{\rm d}x=\int_{x_{1}-tf^{\prime}(M(x_{1},t))}^{x_{2}-tf^{\prime}(m(x_{2},t))}|\varphi_{2}-\varphi_{1}|\,{\rm d}x.

2.
We now prove
(
2.12
)
.
Let the entropy solutions
u_{i}(x,t)
be determined by
E_{i}(u;x,t)
as in
(
2.4
)

with initial data functions
\varphi_{i}(x)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
for
i=1,2
.

To prove
u_{1}(x+,t)\leq u_{2}(x+,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
denote
u^{+}_{1}:=u_{1}(x+,t)
.
Then
(
2.6
)
implies that

E_{1}(u^{+}_{1};x,t)-E_{1}(u;x,t)>0
for any
u<u^{+}_{1}
.
Since
\varphi_{1}(x)\leq\varphi_{2}(x)\ {a.e.}\ {\rm on}\ \mathbb{R}
,
then, if
u<u^{+}_{1}
,

\displaystyle E_{2}(u^{+}_{1};x,t)-E_{2}(u;x,t)

\displaystyle=E_{1}(u^{+}_{1};x,t)-E_{1}(u;x,t)

\displaystyle\quad\,+t\int_{u}^{u_{1}^{+}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle>t\int_{u}^{u_{1}^{+}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s\geq 0.

Since
E_{2}(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum at
u_{2}(x+,t)
, then
u_{2}(x+,t)\geq u_{1}(x+,t)
.

To prove
u_{1}(x-,t)\leq u_{2}(x-,t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
denote
u^{-}_{1}:=u_{1}(x-,t)
. Then
(
2.6
)
implies that

E_{1}(u^{-}_{1};x,t)-E_{1}(u;x,t)\geq 0
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since
\varphi_{1}(x)\leq\varphi_{2}(x)\ {a.e.}\ {\rm on}\ \mathbb{R}
,
then, if
u<u^{-}_{1}
,

\displaystyle E_{2}(u^{-}_{1};x,t)-E_{2}(u;x,t)

\displaystyle=E_{1}(u^{-}_{1};x,t)-E_{1}(u;x,t)

\displaystyle\quad\,+t\int_{u}^{u_{1}^{-}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\geq t\int^{u^{-}_{1}}_{u}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{2}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-\varphi_{1}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))\big)\,{\rm d}s\geq 0.

Since
u^{-}_{2}(x,t)
is the supremum of the set of points at which

E_{2}(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum, then
u_{2}(x-,t)\geq u_{1}(x-,t)
.

3.
We now prove
(
2.13
)
and
(
2.14
)
.

(a).
To prove
(
2.13
)
,
suppose that
\varphi_{n},\varphi\in L^{\infty}
,
and
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n}
is an increasing sequence such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varphi_{n}-\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
.
From
(
2.12
)
,

u_{1}(x+,t)\leq u_{2}(x+,t)\leq\cdots\leq u(x+,t).

Denote
\hat{u}(x,t):=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}(x+,t)
.
Then
\hat{u}(x,t)\leq u(x+,t)
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\hat{u}(x,t)\geq u(x+,t)
.
Let
E_{n}(u;x,t)
and
E(u;x,t)
be given by
(
2.4
)
with
\varphi_{n}(x)
and
\varphi(x)
, respectively.
Then, for any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(2.63)

E(u;x,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u;x,t)\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ u\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
\{u_{n}(x+,t)\}
and
\{\varphi_{n}\}
are both uniformly bounded sequences,
it follows from
(
2.63
)
that,
for any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle E(\hat{u};x,t)-E_{n}(u_{n}^{+};x,t)

\displaystyle=E(\hat{u};x,t)-E_{n}(\hat{u};x,t)

(2.64)

\displaystyle\quad\,+t\int^{\hat{u}}_{u^{+}_{n}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{n}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\,{\rm d}s\rightarrow 0\qquad\,\mbox{as $n\to\infty$},

where
\hat{u}:=\hat{u}(x,t)
and
u_{n}^{+}:=u_{n}(x+,t)
.

On the other hand, from
(
2.6
)
, for every
n
,

E_{n}(u;x,t)\leq E_{n}(u^{+}_{n};x,t)
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
(
2.63
)
–
(
2.64
)
imply that,
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

E(\hat{u};x,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u_{n}^{+};x,t)\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u;x,t)=E(u;x,t),

which means that
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum at
\hat{u}=\hat{u}(x,t)
.
Since
u(x+,t)
is the infimum of the set of points at which
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum,
then
\hat{u}(x,t)\geq u(x+,t)
.

(b).
To prove
(
2.14
)
,
suppose that
\varphi_{n},\varphi\in L^{\infty}
,
and
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n}
is a decreasing sequence such that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|\varphi_{n}-\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}=0
.
From
(
2.12
)
,

u_{1}(x-,t)\geq u_{2}(x-,t)\geq\cdots\geq u(x-,t).

Denote
\check{u}(x,t):=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}(x-,t)
.
Then
\check{u}(x,t)\geq u(x-,t)
.
Thus, it suffices to show that
\check{u}(x,t)\leq u(x-,t)
.
Let
E_{n}(u;x,t)
and
E(u;x,t)
be given by
(
2.4
)
with
\varphi_{n}(x)
and
\varphi(x)
, respectively.
Then, for any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

(2.65)

E(u;x,t)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u;x,t)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ u\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
\{u_{n}(x-,t)\}
and
\{\varphi_{n}\}
are both uniformly bounded sequences,
it follows from
(
2.65
)
that,
for any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

\displaystyle E(\check{u};x,t)-E_{n}(u_{n}^{-};x,t)

\displaystyle=E(\check{u};x,t)-E_{n}(\check{u};x,t)

(2.66)

\displaystyle\quad\,+t\int^{\check{u}}_{u^{-}_{n}}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(\varphi_{n}(x-tf^{\prime}(s))-s\big)\,{\rm d}s\,\rightarrow 0\qquad\,\mbox{as $n\to\infty$},

where
\check{u}:=\check{u}(x,t)
and
u_{n}^{-}:=u_{n}(x-,t)
.

On the other hand, from
(
2.6
)
, for every
n
,

E_{n}(u;x,t)\leq E_{n}(u^{-}_{n};x,t)
holds for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
(
2.65
)
–
(
2.66
)
imply that,
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

E(\check{u};x,t)=\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u^{-}_{n};x,t)\geq\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}E_{n}(u;x,t)=E(u;x,t),

which means that
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum at
\check{u}(x,t)
.
Since
u(x-,t)
is the supremum of the set of points at which
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum,
then
\check{u}(x,t)\leq u(x{-},t)
.

To sum up, we have completed the proof of Corollary
2.5
.

2.3.3.
Proof of Corollary
2.6

The proof is divided into three steps.

1.
We first show that, for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
t>\tau
,

c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)\qquad{\rm if\ and\ only\ if}\qquad c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t).

(a).
Let
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
.
For any
w\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
y(w):=x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(w)

and

u^{-}(w):=u(y(w)-,\tau),\qquad x(w):=y(w)-\tau f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w)).

Then, choosing
v
so that
x=x(w)+tf^{\prime}(v)
,
we have

(2.67)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x-tf^{\prime}(c)=y(c)-\tau f^{\prime}(c),\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle x-tf^{\prime}(v)=y(w)-\tau f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w)),\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle tf^{\prime}(v)=(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(w)+\tau f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w));\end{cases}

see also Fig.
2.11
.
Taking
x_{1}=y(c)
,
x_{2}=y(w)
, and
t=\tau
into
(
2.26
)
leads to

(2.68)

\int_{y(c)}^{y(w)}u(\xi,\tau)\,{\rm d}\xi=\hat{E}(y(c),\tau)-\hat{E}(y(w),\tau).

Since
u(y(w)-,\tau)\in\mathcal{U}(y(w),\tau)
and
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
,
from
(
2.67
)
–
(
2.68
)
,
for any
w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\ E(c;x,t;\tau)-E(w;x,t;\tau)

\displaystyle=(t-\tau)\int_{w}^{c}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\big(u(x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(s),\tau)-s\big)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{y(c)}^{y(w)}u(\xi,\tau)\,{\rm d}\xi-(t-\tau)\int_{w}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\hat{E}(y(c),\tau)-\hat{E}(y(w),\tau)+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\geq E(c;y(c),\tau)-E(u^{-}(w);y(w),\tau)+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\int_{y(c)-\tau f^{\prime}(0)}^{y(w)-\tau f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{y(c)-\tau f^{\prime}(c)}^{y(w)-\tau f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w))}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\tau\int_{c}^{u^{-}(w)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{x-tf^{\prime}(c)}^{x-tf^{\prime}(v)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\tau\int_{c}^{u^{-}(w)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=E(c;x,t)-E(v;x,t)+(t-\tau)\int_{v}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\tau\int_{v}^{u^{-}(w)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

(2.69)

\displaystyle\geq(t-\tau)\int_{v}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\tau\int_{v}^{u^{-}(w)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s\geq 0,

where the last step follows from the convexity of
f(u)
:

\displaystyle\ (t-\tau)\int_{v}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\tau\int_{v}^{u^{-}(w)}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=(t-\tau)\big(sf^{\prime}(s)\big|_{v}^{w}+f(v)-f(w)\big)+\tau\big(sf^{\prime}(s)\big|_{v}^{u^{-}(w)}+f(v)-f(u^{-}(w))\big)

\displaystyle\geq(t-\tau)\big(sf^{\prime}(s)\big|_{v}^{w}+f^{\prime}(w)(v-w)\big)+\tau\big(sf^{\prime}(s)\big|_{v}^{u^{-}(w)}+f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w))(v-u^{-}(w))\big)

\displaystyle=v\big({-}tf^{\prime}(v)+(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(w)+\tau f^{\prime}(u^{-}(w))\big)=0.

This implies that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)
.

Figure 2.11.
The positional relation of
y(w)
and
x(w)
as in
(
2.67
)
.

(b).

Let
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)
.
Then, for any
(x,t)
with
t>\tau
,

E(c;x,t;\tau)-E(w;x,t;\tau)\geq 0\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ any}\ w\in\mathbb{R},

which, by
y(w)=x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(w)
, implies

(2.70)

\int_{y(c)}^{y(w)}(u(\xi,\tau)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi\geq-(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ w\in\mathbb{R}.

Since
y(w)-y(c)=(t-\tau)\big(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(w)\big)
, by the strictly increasing property of
f^{\prime}(u)
,

y(w)-y(c)<0\quad\mbox{if}\ w>c,\qquad\,\,\,y(w)-y(c)>0\quad\mbox{if}\ w<c.

Notice that solution
u(x,t)
has both the left- and right-traces.
It follows from
(
2.70
)
that

\displaystyle u(y(c){-},\tau)-c

\displaystyle=\lim_{w\rightarrow c{+}}\dfrac{1}{y(w)-y(c)}\int_{y(c)}^{y(w)}(u(\xi,\tau)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{w\rightarrow c{+}}\frac{1}{f^{\prime}(w)-f^{\prime}(c)}\int_{c}^{w}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\lim_{w\rightarrow c{+}}\frac{(w-c)f^{\prime}(w)-\big(f(w)-f(c)\big)}{f^{\prime}(w)-f^{\prime}(c)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{w\rightarrow c{+}}\frac{f^{\prime}(w)-f^{\prime}(\zeta)}{f^{\prime}(w)-f^{\prime}(c)}(w-c)=0,

where
\zeta
lies between
w
and
c
.

Similarly, we see that
u(y(c){+},\tau)-c\geq 0
so that

u(y(c){-},\tau)\leq c\leq u(y(c){+},\tau),

which, by
u(y(c){-},\tau)=u^{-}(y(c),\tau)\geq u^{+}(y(c),\tau)=u(y(c){+},\tau)
,
implies that
u(y(c){\pm},\tau)=c
. This means that

\mathcal{U}(y(c),\tau)=\{c\}
.
Then it follows from
(
2.69
)
that,
for any
w\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq E(c;x,t;\tau)-E(w;x,t;\tau)

\displaystyle=\hat{E}(y(c),\tau)-\hat{E}(y(w),\tau)+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle\leq E(c;y(c),\tau)-E(w;y(w),\tau)+(t-\tau)\int_{c}^{w}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\int_{y(c)-\tau f^{\prime}(0)}^{y(w)-\tau f^{\prime}(0)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=-\int_{x-tf^{\prime}(w)}^{x-tf^{\prime}(c)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int_{w}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=E(c;x,t)-E(w;x,t),

which implies that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
.

2.
For any fixed
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
t>\tau
,
choosing
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)=\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)
, then, from
(
2.17
)
,

\hat{E}(x,t;\tau)=E(c;x,t;\tau)+\hat{E}(x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(0),\tau),

which, by using
(
2.68
)
with
w=0
, implies

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,t;\tau)

\displaystyle=-\int_{y(0)}^{y(c)}u(\xi,\tau)\,{\rm d}\xi-(t-\tau)\int_{0}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\hat{E}(x-(t-\tau)f^{\prime}(0),\tau)

\displaystyle=\hat{E}(y(c),\tau)-\hat{E}(y(0),\tau)-(t-\tau)\int_{0}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+\hat{E}(y(0),\tau)

\displaystyle=\hat{E}(y(c),\tau)-(t-\tau)\int_{0}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{x-tf^{\prime}(c)}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-t\int_{0}^{c}sf^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=\hat{E}(x,t).

In particular, by the definitions of
u(x,t)
and
v(x,t)
in
(
2.6
)
and
(
2.17
)
,
respectively, it follows from
\mathcal{U}(x,t;\tau)=\mathcal{U}(x,t)
in
(
2.18
)

that
(
2.19
)
holds.

3.

According to
(
2.11
)
,
to prove that the map
S_{t}
determines a contractive semigroup in the
L^{1}_{\rm loc}
–norm,
it suffices to verify that

S_{t}\varphi=S_{t-\tau}S_{\tau}\varphi
for any
\tau\in[0,t]
.
In fact, this is directly implied by
(
2.19
)
via

S_{t}\varphi=u(\cdot,t)=v(\cdot,t)=S_{t-\tau}u(\cdot,\tau)=S_{t-\tau}S_{\tau}\varphi.

Up to now, we have completed the proof of Corollary
2.6
.

Remark 2.5
.

The function,
E(u;x,t)
, defined by
(
2.4
)
can be understood formally as follows:
It is well known that the local smooth solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
,
if it exists, is determined by the following implicit function of
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

(2.71)

\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(u))-u=0.

If the local smooth solution blows up in a finite time,

(
2.71
)
can not hold exactly as time goes on, because
the characteristics will interact with each other.

Even in this case,
the integral of
\varphi(x-tf^{\prime}(u))-u
with respect to
u
as in
(
2.4
)

can exactly describe the interaction of characteristics in some sense
.
This insight indicates that, as shown in §8,
it is possible to identify the formula for entropy solutions for hyperbolic conservation laws
if such an intrinsic formula for the smooth solutions is known,
which may reveal a deeper connection
between smooth solutions and entropy solutions for hyperbolic conservation laws.

Remark 2.6
.

As shown in Theorem
2.4
, for any point
(x,t)
with
t>0
,

E(u;x,t)
in
(
2.4
)
as a function of
u
attains
its maximum at
u=u(x,t)
in
(
2.8
)
,
which is the unique entropy solution of the Cauchy problem
(
1.1
)
–
(
1.2
)
.
For the subsequent development, based on the analysis of function
E(u;x,t)

and function
\Phi(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

with its convex hull
\bar{\Phi}(x)
over
({-}\infty,\infty)

defined by
(
6.9
)
–
(
6.10
)
,
we can obtain the exact descriptions of the set
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

in Definition
3.1

as the set of the speeds of characteristic lines emitting from
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis,
the lifespan expressions
(
3.37
)
for
t_{p}(x_{0},c)
and
(
3.50
)
for
t_{*}(x_{0},c)

of any characteristic line
L_{c}(x_{0})
emitting from
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis
with speed
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
the set
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
in
(
2.5
)

as the set of the speeds of the shock-free backward characteristics emitting from point
(x,t)
,
the set
\mathcal{D}(x_{0})
in Definition
6.3

as the set of the speeds of the divides emitting from
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis,
and the point set
\mathcal{K}_{0}
in
(
6.11
)
as the set of
the divide generation points.
Using the exact descriptions of sets
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
,

\mathcal{D}(x_{0})
, and
\mathcal{K}_{0}
,
and lifespans
t_{p}(x_{0},c)
and
t_{*}(x_{0},c)
,
we can obtain various fine properties of entropy solutions, especially including
the generation of initial waves for the Cauchy problem,
the structures of entropy solutions inside the backward characteristic triangles,
the formation and development of shocks,
the necessary and sufficient conditions respectively for the divides as well as their locations and speeds,
the global structures of entropy solutions,
and the asymptotic profiles and decay rates respectively in the
L^{\infty}
–norm and the
L^{p}_{{\rm loc}}
–norm.

3.
Characteristics and Initial Waves for the Cauchy Problem

In this section, we focus on the dynamic behaviors of characteristics
and the generation of initial waves of the Cauchy problem.
In §3.1, we establish the criteria for all the cases of
the sets of characteristic generation values in Theorem
3.8

with Propositions
3.4
–
3.7
;
in §3.2, the criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
are established by providing and proving the necessary and sufficient conditions
for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem in Theorem
3.9
.
In §3.3–3.4, we introduce and prove
an upper bound of lifespan and the lifespan of characteristics in Theorems
3.12
–
3.13
,
and provide a classification of characteristics based on the ways
of their terminations in Theorem
3.14
.

3.1.
Generation of characteristics

For any point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
define the line:

(3.1)

L_{c}(x_{0}):=\big\{(x,t)\,:\,x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(c)\,\,\,\mbox{for}\,\,\,t>0\big\}.

Definition 3.1
.

L_{c}(x_{0})
in
(
3.1
)
is called a characteristic
emitting from
x_{0}

if there exists
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
such that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
,
for which
c
is called a characteristic generation value of
x_{0}
.
The point set
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
of characteristic generation values of
x_{0}
is defined by

(3.2)

\mathcal{C}(x_{0}):=\big\{c\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)\,\,\,{\rm for\ some\ }(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})\big\}.

From
(
2.25
)
, it is direct to see that

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\subset[-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}]
.
As shown later in this section,
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
can be determined by

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
and
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
in
(
3.4
)
.
Let

(3.3)

\Phi(x)=\int^{x}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi.

Then the Dini derivatives of
\Phi(x)
at a point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
are given by

(3.4)

\begin{cases}\overline{{\rm D}}_{\pm}\Phi(x_{0}):=\displaystyle\limsup\limits_{y\rightarrow x_{0}{\pm}}\frac{1}{y-x_{0}}\int^{y}_{x_{0}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi=\limsup\limits_{l\rightarrow 0\pm}\frac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi,\\[11.38109pt]
\underline{{\rm D}}_{\pm}\Phi(x_{0}):=\displaystyle\liminf\limits_{y\rightarrow x_{0}{\pm}}\frac{1}{y-x_{0}}\int^{y}_{x_{0}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi=\liminf\limits_{l\rightarrow 0\pm}\frac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi.\end{cases}

For any point
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
,
from
(
2.4
)
–
(
2.6
)
,

c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
if and only if,
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle 0\leq E(c;x,t)-E(u;x,t)=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi+t\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s,

where
l:=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))
.
Equivalently,
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
if and only if,
for any
l=l(u;t,c)
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c):=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi\geq-t\int^{u(l;t,c)}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=:F(l;t,c),

where
u(l;t,c):=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(f^{\prime}(c)-\frac{l}{t})
is the inverse function of

l=l(u;t,c):=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

(i)

c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if there exists
t>0
such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ l\neq 0.

(ii)

c\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if, for any
t>0
,
there exists
l_{n}\neq 0
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}l_{n}=0
such that

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Phi(l_{n};x_{0},c)<F(l_{n};t,c).

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps accordingly.

1
. If
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then, by
(
3.2
)
,
there exists
(x_{1},t_{1})\in L_{c}(x_{0})
such that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{1},t_{1})
,
which, by
(
2.44
)
, implies that,
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t\in(0,t_{1})
,
\mathcal{U}(x,t)=\{c\}
,
i.e.
,

c
is the unique maximum point of
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
.
Then it follows from
(
3.6
)
that
(
3.7
)
holds.

On the other hand, suppose that there exist
t_{1}>0
and small
l_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t_{1},c)\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ l\in({-}l_{0},0)\cup(0,l_{0}).

For any fixed
l
and
c
, it follows from
l=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))
that

tf^{\prime\prime}(u)\,{\rm d}u=(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))\,{\rm d}t
such that

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm d}F}{{\rm d}t}

\displaystyle=-\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s-t(u-c)f^{\prime\prime}(u)\,\frac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}t}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=-\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s+(u-c)\big(f^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(c)\big)=\int^{u}_{c}(u-s)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s>0.

Thus, for any
t<t_{1}
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t_{1},c)>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ l\in({-}l_{0},0)\cup(0,l_{0}).

Since, for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
,

E(\cdot\,;x,t)
attains its maximum on
u\in[-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}]
,
then we can choose
t<t_{1}
sufficiently small such that

l=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))\in({-}l_{0},l_{0})

for any
u\in[-\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}},\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}].

This, by
(
3.10
)
, implies that,
for sufficiently small
t<t_{1}
,

c
is the unique maximum point of
E(\cdot\,;x,t)
for
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
.
This shows that
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
.

2
. It follows from
(
3.7
)
that
c\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if,
for any
t^{\prime}>0
,
there exists a sequence
l_{n}\neq 0
with
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}l_{n}=0
such that

\Phi(l_{n};x_{0},c)\leq F(l_{n};t^{\prime},c)
.
From
(
3.9
)
, for any
t>t^{\prime}
,
F(l_{n};t^{\prime},c)<F(l_{n};t,c)
. Then

\Phi(l_{n};x_{0},c)\leq F(l_{n};t^{\prime},c)<F(l_{n};t,c),

which, by the arbitrariness of
t^{\prime}>0
, implies
(
3.8
)
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, if there exists
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then

(3.11)

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\leq c\leq\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}).

Proof.

Suppose that
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
. Since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,

l=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))
implies that
l(u-c)<0
for
u\neq c

and
u=u(l;t,c)\rightarrow c\mp
as
l\rightarrow 0\pm
.
Then, from
(
3.4
)
and
(
3.6
)
,

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})-c

\displaystyle=\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}\dfrac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi-c\leq\lim_{u\rightarrow c{+}}\frac{1}{f^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(c)}\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\lim_{u\rightarrow c{+}}\frac{f^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(\zeta)}{f^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(c)}(u-c)=0,

where
\zeta
lies between
u
and
c
.

Similarly, it is direct to check that

\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})-c\geq 0.

This implies
(
3.11
)
.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, if
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})>\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
, then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Proposition
3.3
.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, if
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
, then

(3.12)

(\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}))\subset\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\subset[\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})].

Proof.

Denote
a:=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
and
b:=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
. Then
a<b
.

If
c\in(a,b)
, it follows from
(
3.4
)
that,
for any given
\varepsilon
with
0<\varepsilon<\min\{b-c,c-a\}
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{+}}\frac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi>\varepsilon,\qquad\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}\frac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi<-\varepsilon,

so that, for any fixed
t>0
,

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi>0>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ l\neq 0,

which, by
(
3.7
)
, yields
(
3.12
)
.
∎

By Propositions
3.4
–
3.5
,
the remaining task to determine
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
completely is to judge
whether
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

for the case that
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
and
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

for the case that
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.
These are done by Propositions
3.6
–
3.7
below.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.
Denote
a:=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
and
b:=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.
Then

(i)

a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if there exists
t_{1}>0

such that

(3.13)

\Phi(l;x_{0},a)>F(l;t_{1},a)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ l<0.

Furthermore, if there exist some constants
\alpha_{+}\geq 0
and
N_{+}>0
such that

(3.14)

f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N_{+}+o(1))|u-a|^{\alpha_{+}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow a{+},

then
a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

(3.15)

\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}{|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi>-\infty;

or equivalently,
a\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}{|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi=-\infty.

If, in addition, there exist some constants
\gamma_{-}>0
and
C_{\gamma_{-}}\neq 0
such that

(3.16)

\varphi(x_{0}+l)-a=(C_{\gamma_{-}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{-}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{-},

then
a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

(3.17)

C_{\gamma_{-}}>0\qquad{\rm or}\qquad\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})\geq 1;

or equivalently,
a\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

C_{\gamma_{-}}<0
and
\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})<1.

In this case, if
a\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then, for any
t>0
,

(3.18)

\Phi(l;x_{0},a)<F(l;t,a)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ l<0.

(ii)

b\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if
there exists
t_{2}>0
such that

(3.19)

\Phi(l;x_{0},b)>F(l;t_{2},b)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ l>0.

Furthermore, if there exist some constants
\alpha_{-}\geq 0
and
N_{-}>0

such that

(3.20)

f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N_{-}+o(1))|u-b|^{\alpha_{-}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow b{-},

then
b\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

(3.21)

\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{+}}{|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{-}}{1+\alpha_{-}}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-b)\,{\rm d}\xi>-\infty;

or equivalently,
b\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{+}}{|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{-}}{1+\alpha_{-}}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-b)\,{\rm d}\xi=-\infty.

If, in addition, there exist some constants
\gamma_{+}>0
and
C_{\gamma_{+}}\neq 0
such that

(3.22)

\varphi(x_{0}+l)-b=(C_{\gamma_{+}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{+}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{+},

then
b\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

(3.23)

C_{\gamma_{+}}>0\qquad{\rm or}\qquad\gamma_{+}(1+\alpha_{-})\geq 1;

or equivalently,
b\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if

C_{\gamma_{+}}<0
and
\gamma_{+}(1+\alpha_{-})<1.

In this case, if
b\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then, for any
t>0
,

(3.24)

\Phi(l;x_{0},b)<F(l;t,b)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ l>0.

Proof.

We divide the proof into two steps accordingly.

1
. From Lemma
3.2
,
a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
if and only if
(
3.7
)
holds.

(a)
.
To prove
(
3.13
)
, it suffices to show that,
for any fixed
t>0
,
(
3.13
)
always holds for small
l>0
.
In fact, since
a<b
, it follows from
(
3.4
)
that,
for any given
\varepsilon\in(0,b-a)
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{+}}\frac{1}{l}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi=b-a>\varepsilon,

which implies that, for any fixed
t>0
,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},a)=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi>0>F(l;t,a)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ l>0.

(b)
. Assume that
(
3.14
)
holds.
For any fixed
t>0
, it follows from
l=t(f^{\prime}(a)-f^{\prime}(u))
that

u=u(l;t,a)\rightarrow a{+}
as
l\rightarrow 0{-}
.
From
(
3.6
)
and
(
A.5
)
–
(
A.6
)
,
for small
l<0
,

\displaystyle F(l;t,a)

\displaystyle=-t\int^{u(l;t,a)}_{a}(s-a)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=-t\frac{N_{+}+o(1)}{2+\alpha_{+}}|u-a|^{2+\alpha_{+}}

\displaystyle=-t\frac{N_{+}+o(1)}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}+o(1)}\big|f^{\prime}(u)-f^{\prime}(a)\big|\Big)^{\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}

\displaystyle=-t\frac{N_{+}+o(1)}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}+o(1)}\frac{|l|}{t}\Big)^{\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}

(3.26)

\displaystyle=-(1+o(1))\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,t^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,|l|^{\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}.

If
a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then it follows from
(
3.13
)
and
(
3.26
)
that

\displaystyle\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi

\displaystyle=\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,\Phi(l;x_{0},a)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,t_{1}^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}>-\infty.

On the other hand, if
(
3.15
)
holds, there exists
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{-}}|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi\geq-\frac{M}{4},

which, by combining with
(
3.26
)
, implies that, for small
l<0
,

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},a)=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi>-\frac{M}{2}\,|l|^{\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}>F(l;\tilde{t}_{1},a)

as desired, where

\tilde{t}_{1}
is given by

\tilde{t}_{1}:=\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}}\big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{2+\alpha_{+}}\frac{1}{M}\big)^{1+\alpha_{+}}.

(c)
.
If, in addition,
(
3.16
)
holds, then, by a simple calculation,

(3.27)

\displaystyle|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{+}}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi=\frac{C_{\gamma_{-}}+o(1)}{1+\gamma_{-}}|l|^{\frac{\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})-1}{1+\alpha_{+}}},

which implies that
(
3.15
)
holds if and only if

C_{\gamma_{-}}>0
or
\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})\geq 1
.
Then
(
3.17
)
holds.
It is direct to check from
(
3.26
)
–
(
3.27
)
that
(
3.18
)
holds.

2
. By the same arguments as in Step 1 above, it can be checked that
(
3.19
)
–
(
3.24
)
hold.
∎

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=:a
.
Then

(3.28)

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\{a\}\qquad{\rm or}\qquad\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing.

Moreover, the following properties are valid
:

(i)

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\{a\}
if and only if there exists
t_{1}>0
such that

(3.29)

\Phi(l;x_{0},a)>F(l;t_{1},a)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ l\neq 0.

(ii)

If there exist some constants
\alpha_{\pm}\geq 0
and
N_{\pm}>0
such that

(3.30)

f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N_{\pm}+o(1))|u-a|^{\alpha_{\pm}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow a{\pm},

then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
if and only if
at least one of the following two limits holds
:

(3.31)

\mathop{\underline{\lim}}\limits_{l\rightarrow 0{\pm}}|l|^{-\frac{2+\alpha_{\mp}}{1+\alpha_{\mp}}}\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-a)\,{\rm d}\xi=-\infty.

(iii)

If, in addition, there exist some constants
\gamma_{\pm}>0
and
C_{\gamma_{\pm}}\neq 0
such that

(3.32)

\varphi(x_{0}+l)-a=\big(C_{\gamma_{\pm}}+o(1)\big){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{\pm}}\qquad\,\,{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{\pm},

then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
if and only if
at least one of the following two holds
:

(3.33)

C_{\gamma_{\pm}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\gamma_{\pm}(1+\alpha_{\mp})<1.

In this case, if
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
, then, for any
t>0
,
at least one of the following two holds
:

\Phi(l;x_{0},a)<F(l;t,a)\qquad\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0.

Proof.

It follows immediately by letting
b=a
in Proposition
3.6
.
∎

To sum up, for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
the point set
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
of characteristic generation values of
x_{0}

can be completely determined by Theorem
3.8
as follows:

Theorem 3.8

(Criteria for all the cases of
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
)
.

Let the point set
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
of characteristic generation values of
x_{0}
,
and the Dini derivatives
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
and
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
at
x_{0}

be given by
(
3.2
)
and
(
3.3
)
–
(
3.4
)
, respectively.

(i)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})>\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
.

(ii)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
then
(
3.12
)
holds,
i.e.
,

(\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}))\subset\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\subset[\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})].

Moreover, the following properties are valid
:

(a)

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\ (resp.,\ \underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}))

if and only if
\eqref{c4.6}\ (resp.,\ \eqref{c4.8})
holds
;

(b)

For
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
,
if
(
3.14
)
holds, then
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

if and only if
(
3.15
)
holds
and, if in addition
(
3.16
)
holds,
then
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

if and only if
(
3.17
)
holds
;

(c)

For
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
if
(
3.20
)
holds,
then
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

if and only if
(
3.21
)
holds and,
if in addition
(
3.22
)
holds,
then
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

if and only if
(
3.23
)
holds.

(iii)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
then
(
3.28
)
holds,
i.e.
,

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\big\{\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\big\}\qquad{\rm or}\qquad\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing.

Moreover, the following properties are valid
:

(a)

\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

if and only if
(
3.29
)
holds
;

(b)

If
(
3.30
)
holds,
then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
if and only if
at least one case of
(
3.31
)
holds
;

(c)

If
(
3.30
)
and
(
3.32
)
hold,
then
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
if and only if
at least one case of
(
3.33
)
holds.

As a summative theorem, it follows directly from Propositions
3.4
–
3.7
.

3.2.
Generation of all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem

Combining Theorem
3.8
with Propositions
3.4
–
3.7
together,
we obtain the criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
emitting from any point
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis as follows:

Theorem 3.9

(Classification of the initial waves for the Cauchy problem)
.

For any point
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis,
the initial waves for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
must be one of the following six cases
:

(i)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})>\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
or
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

with
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
is a shock
\mathcal{S}
;

(ii)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

with
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\not=\varnothing
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
is a characteristic
;

(iii)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

and
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=[\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})]
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
is a rarefaction wave
\mathcal{R}
;

(iv)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

and
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=(\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})]
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
is
\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{R}
;

(v)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

and
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=[\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}))
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem
emitting from
x_{0}
is
\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{S}
;

(vi)

If
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

and
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=(\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}))
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from
x_{0}
is
\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{R}+\mathcal{S}
.

In the above,
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
, and
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

are given by
(
3.2
)
–
(
3.4
)
.
See
Fig.

2.2
and
Fig.

3.1
for the details.

Figure 3.1.
a=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\geq\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=b
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps.

1
. From
(
3.11
)
,
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})>\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

implies that
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
.
Now suppose
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
.
For any
t=t_{1}>0
, we let

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle a(t_{1})=\sup\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x{-},t_{1}))<x_{0}\big\},\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle b(t_{1})\,=\,\inf\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x{-},t_{1}))>x_{0}\big\}.\end{cases}

Then
a(t_{1})\leq b(t_{1})
.
If
a(t_{1})<b(t_{1})
, for any
x_{1}\in(a(t_{1}),b(t_{1}))
,

x_{1}-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x_{1}{-},t_{1}))=x_{0}
so that
u(x_{1}{-},t_{1})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
which contradicts with
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
.
Therefore,
a(t_{1})=b(t_{1})
.

(a)
. Set
x=x(t):=a(t)
for
t>0
. Then
x(t)
is a single-valued curve with
x(0)=x_{0}
.
From
(
2.42
)
, for any
t_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x(t_{1})-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){-},t_{1}))=\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow x(t_{1})-}\big(x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x{-},t_{1}))\big)\leq x_{0},\\
\displaystyle x(t_{1})-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1}))=\lim\limits_{x\rightarrow x(t_{1})+}\big(x-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x{-},t_{1}))\big)\geq x_{0}.\end{cases}

Then, from
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})=\varnothing
,

(3.34)

x(t_{1})-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){-},t_{1}))<x_{0}<x(t_{1})-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1})),

so that, by the strictly increasing property of
f^{\prime}(u)
,
u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1})<u(x(t_{1}){-},t_{1})
,
which implies that the entropy solution
u(x,t)
is discontinuous along the single-valued curve
x=x(t)
.

(b)
. We now prove the continuity of
x=x(t)
.
Denote
y^{\pm}(x(t),t):=x(t)-tf^{\prime}(u(x(t){\pm},t))
.
From
(
2.44
)
and
(
3.34
)
, for any
t_{2}>t_{1}>0
,

(3.35)

y^{-}(x(t_{2}),t_{2})\leq y^{-}(x(t_{1}),t_{1})\leq y^{+}(x(t_{1}),t_{1})\leq y^{+}(x(t_{2}),t_{2}).

Thus,
y^{-}(x(t),t)
is nonincreasing and
y^{+}(x(t),t)
is nondecreasing.
Letting
t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1}{+}
in
(
3.35
)
,

y^{-}(\overline{x(t_{1}+)},t_{1})\leq y^{-}(x(t_{1}),t_{1})\leq y^{+}(x(t_{1}),t_{1})\leq y^{+}(\underline{x(t_{1}+)},t_{1}),

where
\overline{x(t_{1}+)}:=\mathop{\overline{\lim}}_{t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1}{+}}x(t_{2})

and
\underline{x(t_{1}+)}:=\mathop{\underline{\lim}}_{t_{2}\rightarrow t_{1}{+}}x(t_{2})
.

By Lemma
2.8
,
Since
y^{\pm}(\cdot,t_{1})
are nondecreasing,
then
\overline{x(t_{1}+)}\leq x(t_{1})\leq\underline{x(t_{1}+)},

which implies that

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{1}{+}}x(t)=x(t_{1})
for any
t_{1}>0
.
Thus,
x(t)
is right-continuous on
(0,\infty)
.

Similarly, letting
t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}{-}
in
(
3.35
)
,

y^{-}(x(t_{2}),t_{2})\leq y^{-}(\underline{x(t_{2}-)},t_{2})\leq y^{+}(\overline{x(t_{2}-)},t_{2})\leq y^{+}(x(t_{2}),t_{2}),

where
\overline{x(t_{2}-)}:=\mathop{\overline{\lim}}_{t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}{-}}x(t_{1})

and
\underline{x(t_{2}-)}:=\mathop{\underline{\lim}}_{t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}{-}}x(t_{1})
.
This, together with Lemma
2.8
, implies that

\overline{x(t_{2}-)}\leq x(t_{2})\leq\underline{x(t_{2}-)}
so that

x(t)
is left-continuous on
(0,\infty)
.

Therefore,
x=x(t)
is continuous on
(0,\infty)
.
Meanwhile, letting
t_{1}\rightarrow 0{+}
in
(
3.34
)
,
we see that
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0{+}}x(t)=x_{0}
.

2
. Denote
a:=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
.
Since
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\not=\varnothing
, by Proposition
3.3
,
there exists only one characteristic emitting from
x_{0}
,
which is given by
x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
with
t\in[0,t_{0})
for some
t_{0}>0
.
This means that there exists no rarefaction wave emitting from
x_{0}
.

We now prove that there does not exist a shock emitting from
x_{0}
.
Otherwise, there exists a shock
x=x(t)
emitting from
x_{0}
.
By
(
2.44
)
, shock
x=x(t)
cannot intersect
the characteristic:
x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
on
(0,t_{0})
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
x(t)<x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
on
(0,t_{0})
.
Since
x(0)=x_{0}
, then
x(t)-tf^{\prime}(u(x(t){+},t))\geq x_{0}
. Since, for any
t_{1}\in(0,t_{0})
,
the shock-free characteristic
x=x(t_{1})+(t-t_{1})f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1}))

cannot intersect the shock-free characteristic
x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
on
(0,t_{1})
, then

x(t_{1})-t_{1}f^{\prime}(u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1}))=x_{0},

which implies that
u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
.
Since
x(t)<x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
on
(0,t_{0})
,
then

u(x(t_{1}){+},t_{1})<a=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),

which contradicts to Proposition
3.3
.

3
.
For the remaining four cases, by Propositions
3.4
–
3.7
,
it suffices to prove that, when
a=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=b
,

a\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
(
resp.
,

b\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
)
if and only if
there exists a shock emitting from
x_{0}

and lying on the left
(
resp.
, right
)
of the rarefaction wave emitting from
x_{0}
.
In fact, if there exists a shock emitting from
x_{0}

and lying on the left of the rarefaction wave emitting from
x_{0}
,
then
a\notin\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
.
If
a\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, by the same arguments as (ii),
there exists no shock emitting from
x_{0}

and lying on the left of the characteristic:
x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(a)
.
∎

Applying the solution formula
(
2.8
)
for the Cauchy problem
(
2.15
)

with the initial data function
u(x,\tau)

as shown in
(
2.16
)
–
(
2.17
)
.
We define
\mathcal{C}_{\tau}(x)
similar to
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})

as in
(
3.2
)
,
and
\Phi_{\tau}(x):=\int_{0}^{x}u(\xi,\tau)\,{\rm d}\xi

similar to
\Phi(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi

on the real line
t=\tau
for any
\tau>0
.

Since
u(x{-},\tau)
and
u(x{+},\tau)
always exist, then

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi_{\tau}(x)=u(x{-},\tau)\geq u(x{+},\tau)=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi_{\tau}(x).

Combining Theorem
3.9
with Corollary
2.6
,
the initial waves for the Cauchy problem emitting the line
t=\tau
for any
\tau>0
are as follows:

Corollary 3.10
.

The initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from any point
(x,t)
with
t>0

must be one of the following two waves
:

(i)

If
u(x{-},t)>u(x{+},t)
, or
u(x{-},t)=u(x{+},t)
with
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x)=\varnothing
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from point
(x,t)
is a shock
\mathcal{S}
;

(ii)

If
u(x{-},t)=u(x{+},t)
with
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x)=\{u(x{-},t)\}
,
then the initial wave for the Cauchy problem emitting from point
(x,t)
is a characteristic.

3.3.
Lifespan of characteristics

In this subsection, we introduce a upper bound of lifespan and the lifespan of characteristics.

For any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
we define a upper bound of lifespan

t_{p}:=t_{p}(x_{0},c)
of characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
defined by
(
3.1
)
as follows:

(3.36)

\displaystyle t_{p}=t_{p}(x_{0},c):=\min\big\{t_{p}^{\pm}(x_{0},c)\big\},

where
t_{p}^{\pm}:=t_{p}^{\pm}(x_{0},c)
are defined by

(3.37)

\displaystyle t_{p}^{\pm}=t_{p}^{\pm}(x_{0},c):=\sup\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,:\,\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t,c)\quad\,\,\,{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0\big\}

with
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)
and
F(l;t,c)
given by
(
3.6
)
, and
\pm l
as the symbol representing
+l
and
-l
together
from now on.

Since
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
combining
(
3.36
)
–
(
3.37
)
with Lemma
3.2
yields that

t_{p}^{\pm}>0
,
i.e.
,
t_{p}^{\pm}\in(0,\infty]
,
which implies that
t_{p}\in(0,\infty]
.

From
(
3.6
)
,
F(l;t,c)<0
for any
l\neq 0
and
t>0
.
Therefore, if
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq 0
for small
\pm l>0
,
then
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t,c)
for small
\pm l>0
and
t>0
,
which means that
t_{p}^{\pm}=\infty
.
Otherwise,
t_{p}^{\pm}
belong to
(0,\infty)
or equal to
\infty
.

Lemma 3.11
.

For characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})

defined by
(
3.1
)

with
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
if
t_{p}^{\pm}\in(0,\infty)
, then

(i)

For
t<t_{p}^{\pm}
,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0.

(ii)

For
t=t_{p}^{\pm}
,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq F(l;t_{p}^{\pm},c)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0.

(iii)

For
t>t_{p}^{+}\,(\mbox{or}\,\,\,t_{p}^{-})
,
there exists
a sequence

l_{n}^{+}>0\,(\mbox{or}\,\,\,l_{n}^{-}<0)
with

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}l_{n}^{\pm}=0
such that

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Phi(l_{n}^{\pm};x_{0},c)<F(l_{n}^{\pm};t,c).

In the above,
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)
and
F(l;t,c)
are given by
(
3.6
)
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps correspondingly.

1
. For
t<t_{p}^{\pm}
,
(
3.38
)
is directly implied by the definition of
t_{p}^{\pm}
in
(
3.37
)
.

2
. For
t=t_{p}^{\pm}
, from the continuity of
F(l;\cdot,c)
inferred by
(
3.9
)
,
letting
t\rightarrow(t_{p}^{\pm})-
in
(
3.38
)
, we obtain
(
3.39
)
.

3
. For
t>t_{p}^{\pm}
, we prove
(
3.40
)
by contradiction.
In fact, if
(
3.40
)
does not hold, there exists
l_{0}>0
such that

\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq F(l;t,c)
for
\pm l\in(0,l_{0}).

\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for}\ \pm l\in(0,l_{0}).

Since
F(l;\cdot,c)
is strictly increasing which is inferred by
(
3.9
)
,
then, for any
t^{\prime}\in(t_{p}^{\pm},t)
,

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq F(l;t,c)>F(l;t^{\prime},c)\qquad{\rm for}\ \pm l\in(0,l_{0}),

which contradicts to the definition of
t_{p}^{\pm}
in
(
3.37
)
.
∎

In order to determine the exact values of
t_{p}^{\pm}
, we assume that

(3.41)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N_{\pm}+o(1))|u-c|^{\alpha_{\pm}}\qquad&{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow c{\pm},\\[5.69054pt]
\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{\pm}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{\pm}}\qquad&{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{\pm},\end{cases}

for some constants
\alpha_{\pm}\geq 0,\gamma_{\pm}>0
,
N_{\pm}>0
, and
C_{\gamma_{\pm}}\neq 0
.

For characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
with
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
the exact values of
t_{p}^{\pm}
can be determined as follows:

Theorem 3.12

(A upper bound of lifespan of characteristics)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\neq\varnothing
.
For any characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
defined by
(
3.1
)
with
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,

t_{p}^{\pm}=t_{p}^{\pm}(x_{0},c)>0
. There are two cases
:

(i)

Case
:

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.

(a)

If
c\in(\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0}),\,\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0}))
, then
t_{p}=t_{p}^{\pm}=\infty
.

(b)

If
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then
t_{p}^{+}=\infty
.
Assume that
(
3.41
)
holds for some constants
\alpha_{+}\geq 0,\gamma_{-}>0
,

N_{+}>0
, and
C_{\gamma_{-}}\neq 0
,
then
t^{-}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(3.42)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{-}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\quad\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})=1.

Furthermore, if
t^{-}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

(3.43)

\displaystyle t^{-}_{p}=\big(\gamma_{-}N_{+}|C_{\gamma_{-}}|^{1+\alpha_{+}}\big)^{-1},

and, for any
t>t_{p}^{-}
,
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)<F(l;t,c)
for small
l<0
.

(c)

If
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
, then
t_{p}^{-}=\infty
.
Assume that
(
3.41
)
holds for some constants
\alpha_{-}\geq 0,\gamma_{+}>0
,

N_{-}>0
, and
C_{\gamma_{+}}\neq 0
,
then
t^{+}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(3.44)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{+}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\quad\gamma_{+}(1+\alpha_{-})=1.

Furthermore, if
t^{+}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

(3.45)

\displaystyle t^{+}_{p}=\big(\gamma_{+}N_{-}|C_{\gamma_{+}}|^{1+\alpha_{-}}\big)^{-1},

and, for any
t>t_{p}^{+}
,
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)<F(l;t,c)
for small
l>0
.

(ii)

Case
:

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.
Assume that
(
3.41
)
holds,
then
t^{\pm}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(3.46)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{\pm}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\quad\gamma_{\pm}(1+\alpha_{\mp})=1.

Furthermore, if
t^{\pm}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

(3.47)

\displaystyle t^{\pm}_{p}=\big(\gamma_{\pm}N_{\mp}|C_{\gamma_{\pm}}|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}}\big)^{-1},

and for any
t>t_{p}^{\pm}
,
\Phi(l;x_{0},c)<F(l;t,c)
for small
\pm l>0
.

In the above,

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,

Proof.

1

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)=\int^{x_{0}+l}_{x_{0}}(\varphi(\xi)-c)\,{\rm d}\xi>0>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\Phi(l;x_{0},c)=(1+o(1))\frac{C_{\gamma_{-}}}{1+\gamma_{-}}|l|^{1+\gamma_{-}},\\[5.69054pt]
F(l;t,c)=-(1+o(1))\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,t^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}\,|l|^{1+\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle t^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}<(1+o(1))\frac{1+\gamma_{-}}{|C_{\gamma_{-}}|}\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{2+\alpha_{+}}\Big(\frac{1+\alpha_{+}}{N_{+}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}}=(1+o(1))\big(\gamma_{-}N_{+}|C_{\gamma_{-}}|^{1+\alpha_{+}}\big)^{-\frac{1}{1+\alpha_{+}}},

\Phi(l;x_{0},c)<F(l;t,c)
for small

(c).

)
–

hold.

.

From

, for any

\displaystyle t_{*}=t_{*}(x_{0},c)

\displaystyle:=\sup\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,:\,c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)\quad\,\,\,{\rm for}\ (x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})\big\}

(3.50)

\displaystyle\;=\sup\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,:\,\Phi(l;x_{0},c)\geq F(l;t,c)\quad\,\,\,{\rm for\ all}\,\,l=l(u;t,c)\big\},

where

)

and

in

that

.

Theorem 3.13

(Lifespan of characteristics)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})\neq\varnothing
. If
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
then
t_{*}=t_{*}(x_{0},c)
given by
(
3.50
)

is the lifespan of characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})

given by

(
3.1
)

in the following sense
:

(i)

If
t_{*}<\infty
,
then, for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
,

(3.51)

\displaystyle\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x,t)\,\,\,{\rm if}\ t<t_{*},\quad\,\{c\}\subset\mathcal{U}(x_{*},t_{*})\,\,\,{\rm if}\ t=t_{*},\quad\,c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)\,\,\,{\rm if}\ t>t_{*},

where
x_{*}:=x_{0}+t_{*}f^{\prime}(c)
.

(ii)

If
t_{*}=\infty
, then

(3.52)

\displaystyle\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x,t)\qquad\mbox{{\rm for\ any} $(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})$ {\rm with}\ $t>0$}.

Proof.

From
(
2.44
)
, if
c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{1},t_{1})
for some
t_{1}>0
,
then
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t\in(0,t_{1})
so that,
the set

\displaystyle T(x_{0},c):=\big\{t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\,:\,c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)\quad{\rm for}\ (x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})\big\}

is an interval with
0=\inf T(x_{0},c)
.

1
. For
t_{*}<\infty
, since
T(x_{0},c)
is an interval with
0=\inf T(x_{0},c)
and
t_{*}=\sup T(x_{0},c)
,
then
(0,t_{*})\subset T(x_{0},c)\subset(0,t_{*}]
,
which, by
(
2.41
)
, implies that
c=\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{*}{-}}u(x,t)\in\mathcal{U}(x_{*},t_{*})
,
where the limit is taken along line
L_{c}(x_{0})
.
Then, by
(
2.44
)
,
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t\in(0,t_{*})
.
Thus,
(
3.51
)
holds for
t\leq t_{*}
.
For
t>t_{*}
,
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)
follows immediately from the definition of
t_{*}
in
(
3.50
)
.

2
. For
t_{*}=\infty
, since
T(x_{0},c)
is an interval with
0=\inf T(x_{0},c)
and
t_{*}=\sup T(x_{0},c)
,
then
T(x_{0},c)=(0,\infty)
,
which means that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t>0
.
We prove
(
3.52
)
by contradiction.
Otherwise, if there exists
(x_{1},t_{1})\in L_{c}(x_{0})
such that
\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{1},t_{1})
,
i.e.
,
there exists
u_{1}\in\mathcal{U}(x_{1},t_{1})
with
u_{1}\neq c
satisfying

(3.53)

\displaystyle\Phi(l_{1};x_{0},c)=F(l_{1};t_{1},c)\qquad{\rm with}\ l_{1}:=l(u_{1};t_{1},c).

Since
l_{1}=t_{1}(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u_{1}))
and
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,
then, for any
t>t_{1}
, there exists
\hat{u}_{1}
lying between
c
and
u_{1}

such that
l_{1}=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(\hat{u}_{1}))
. From
(
3.9
)
,

F(l_{1};\cdot,c)
is strictly increasing, then, by
(
3.53
)
,

\Phi(l_{1};x_{0},c)=F(l_{1};t_{1},c)<F(l_{1};t,c)
for any
t>t_{1},

which implies that
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t>t_{1}
.
This contradicts to
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t>0
.
∎

3.4.
Termination of characteristics

In this subsection, using the upper bound of lifespan and the lifespan,
we provide a classification of characteristics by the way of their terminations.

Combining
(
3.50
)
with
(
3.36
)
–
(
3.37
)
,
it is direct to see that

(3.54)

\displaystyle t_{*}=t_{*}(x_{0},c)\leq t_{p}(x_{0},c)=\min\big\{t^{\pm}_{p}(x_{0},c)\big\}=\min\big\{t_{p}^{\pm}\big\}.

Thus, if
t_{p}=\infty
, then
t_{*}<t_{p}=\infty
or
t_{*}=t_{p}=\infty
;
and if
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
,
then
(x_{p},t_{p})\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
x_{p}:=x_{0}+t_{p}f^{\prime}(c)
and

(a)

If
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
t_{*}<t_{p}
by
(
3.51
)
.

(b)

If
c\in\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
t_{*}=t_{p}
by
(
3.51
)
and
(
3.54
)
.
There exist two cases:

\displaystyle\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})\qquad{\rm or}\qquad\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p}).

Finally, we present a classification of characteristics by the way of their terminations.

Theorem 3.14
.

Any characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
defined by
(
3.1
)

emitting from point
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis
must be one of the following four cases
:

(i)

For
t_{*}=t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
with
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
terminates at
(x_{p},t_{p})

only due to the compression of local characteristics near
x_{0}
,
and
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a continuous shock generation point.

(ii)

For
t_{*}=t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
with
\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
terminates at
(x_{p},t_{p})

due to the combination of the compression of local characteristics near
x_{0}
for parts
with some characteristics away from
x_{0}
,
and
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a discontinuous shock generation point or a point of shocks.

(iii)

For
t_{*}<t_{p}\in(0,\infty]
,
characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
terminates at
(x_{*},t_{*})

due to the collision of
L_{c}(x_{0})
with some characteristics
away from
x_{0}
, and
(x_{*},t_{*})
is a point of a shock.

(iv)

For
t_{*}=t_{p}=\infty
, characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
exists for all time.

In the above,
t_{p}
and
t_{*}
are defined by

(
3.36
)
and
(
3.50
)
, respectively.
See
Fig.
3.3
for the details.

Figure 3.3.
Four types of characteristics classified by
t_{*}
and
t_{p}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into four steps correspondingly.

1
. For
t_{*}=t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
with
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
it follows from
(
3.51
)
that
L_{c}(x_{0})
is shock-free for
t\leq t_{p}
.

\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
implies that solution
u=u(x,t)
is continuous at
(x_{p},t_{p})
.
By Corollary
3.10
(i), to prove that
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a continuous shock generation point,
it suffices to show that
\mathcal{C}_{t_{p}}(x_{p})=\varnothing

or equivalently
c\notin\mathcal{C}_{t_{p}}(x_{p})
.
Otherwise,
c\in\mathcal{C}_{t_{p}}(x_{p})
so that
there exists
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t>t_{p}
such that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t;t_{p})
,
which, by Corollary
2.6
, implies that
c\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
.
This contradicts to
(
3.51
)
that
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)
if
t>t_{*}=t_{p}
.

2
. For
t_{*}=t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
with
\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
,
it follows from
(
3.51
)
that
L_{c}(x_{0})
is shock-free for
t<t_{p}
.

\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
implies that solution
u=u(x,t)
is discontinuous at
(x_{p},t_{p})
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
2.9
(iii),
if
\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})=[u(x_{p}+,t_{p}),u(x_{p}-,t_{p})]
,
then point
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a discontinuous shock generation point;
and if
\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})\subsetneqq[u(x_{p}+,t_{p}),u(x_{p}-,t_{p})]
,
then point
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a point of shocks.

3
. For
t_{*}<t_{p}\in(0,\infty]
,
it follows from
(
3.51
)
that
L_{c}(x_{0})
is shock-free for
t<t_{*}
.
It suffices to show that
\{c\}\subsetneqq\mathcal{U}(x_{*},t_{*})
,
which is proved by contradiction.
Otherwise, assume that
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{*},t_{*})
.
From
(
3.51
)
,
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)
if
t>t_{*}
,
then there exists
u(t)\neq c
such that

u(t)\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
for any
(x,t)\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
t>t_{*}
.
From
(
2.41
)
, it follows from
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{*},t_{*})
that

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{*}{+}}u(t)=c
, then

(3.55)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{*}{+}}l(t):=\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{*}{+}}t\big(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u(t))\big)=0.

On the other hand, from
(
3.38
)
, for any
t_{1}\in[t_{*},t_{p})
,
there exists small
l_{1}>0
such that

\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t_{1},c)
for
l\in({-}l_{1},0)\cup(0,l_{1})
.
This, by
(
3.9
)
, implies that, for any
t\in[t_{*},t_{1})
,

(3.56)

\displaystyle\Phi(l;x_{0},c)>F(l;t_{1},c)>F(l;t,c)\qquad{\rm for}\ l\in({-}l_{1},0)\cup(0,l_{1}).

Since
c\notin\mathcal{U}(x,t)
and
u(t)\in\mathcal{U}(x,t)
,
then
E(c;x,t)-E(u(t);x,t)<0
,
which, by
(
3.6
)
and
(
3.56
)
, implies that

l(t)=t(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u(t)))\notin({-}l_{1},l_{1}).

This contradicts to
(
3.55
)
.

4
. It follows from
(
3.52
)
immediately.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

From Proposition
3.6
–
3.7
,
the necessary and sufficient conditions for the rays emitting from points on the
x
–axis to be characteristics,
at least local-in-time, hold for all the points,

even when the point on the
x
–axis is not a Lebesgue point of the initial data function.
Owing to the completeness of such results,
without
a priori
assumptions on the continuity, or the traces,
or the property of the approximating jump of the initial data function,
we can obtain the criteria for all the six types of initial waves for the Cauchy problem
with the flux functions satisfying
(
1.3
)
and the initial data only in
L^{\infty}
.

4.
New Features of Formation and Development of Shocks

In this section, we study the formation and development of shocks.
In §4.1, we discover all the five types of continuous shock generation points
and prove the necessary and sufficient conditions of them in Theorem
4.1
;
in §4.2, we prove the optimal regularities of shock curves and entropy solutions
near all the five types of continuous shock generation points in Theorems
4.4
,
in which the continuous shock generation points are not necessarily assumed to be isolated.

4.1.
Formation of shocks
for all the five types of continuous shock generation points

By Theorem
3.14
,
a continuous shock generation point
(x,t)

must be a point
(x_{p},t_{p})
of some characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
,
i.e.
,

(4.1)

\displaystyle x=x_{p}=x_{0}+t_{p}f^{\prime}(c),\qquad t=t_{p}\in(0,\infty).

Moreover,
point
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a continuous shock generation point if and only if

t_{*}=t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
and
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p}),

which, by
t_{*}\leq t_{p}
in
(
3.54
)

and the fact that
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
implying
t_{*}\geq t_{p}
,
is equivalent to

(4.2)

\displaystyle t_{p}\in(0,\infty)\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p}).

Furthermore, from Theorem
3.12
,
the necessary condition of
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
of characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
is

c\in\partial\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
where
\partial\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
is the boundary of
\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
.

As shown in Theorem
3.12
, in order to determine the exact values of
t_{p}^{\pm}
,
we need the information of
f^{\prime\prime}(u)
near
c
and
\varphi(x)
near
x_{0}
. Assume that

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N_{\pm}+o(1))|u-c|^{\alpha_{\pm}}\qquad&{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow c{\pm},\\[2.84526pt]
\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{\pm}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{\pm}}\qquad&{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{\pm},\end{cases}

for some constants
\alpha_{\pm}\geq 0,\gamma_{\pm}>0
,
N_{\pm}>0
, and
C_{\gamma_{\pm}}\neq 0
.

Theorem 4.1

(Formation of the shocks with continuous shock generation point)
.

A continuous shock generation point
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}

must be a point
(x_{p},t_{p})
as in
(
4.1
)
of some characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})

defined by
(
3.1
)
.
Moreover, point
(x_{p},t_{p})

is a continuous shock generation point if and only if

t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
and

(4.4)

\displaystyle\Phi(x_{0}+l)-\Phi(x_{0})-c\,l>\big(\rho((f^{\prime})^{-1}(f^{\prime}(c)-l/t_{p}),c)-c\big)\,l\qquad{\rm for\ all}\ l\neq 0,

where
l=t_{p}(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))
with
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\Phi(x)=\int^{x}_{0}\varphi(\xi)\,{\rm d}\xi
,
and
\rho(u,c)
is given by
(
A.1
)
.
Furthermore, if

(
4.3
)
is assumed,
then the following statements hold
:

(i)

For
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
there exist two cases
:

(a)

If
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
then
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(4.5)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{-}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha_{+})=1,

and, if
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

t_{p}=t^{-}_{p}=(\gamma_{-}N_{+}|C_{\gamma_{-}}|^{1+\alpha_{+}})^{-1}.

(b)

If
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
then
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(4.6)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{+}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\gamma_{+}(1+\alpha_{-})=1,

and, if
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

t_{p}=t^{+}_{p}=(\gamma_{+}N_{-}|C_{\gamma_{+}}|^{1+\alpha_{-}})^{-1}.

(ii)

For
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
then
t^{\pm}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

(4.7)

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{\pm}}<0\qquad{\rm and}\quad\gamma_{\pm}(1+\alpha_{\mp})=1,

and, if
t^{\pm}_{p}\in(0,\infty)
, then

t^{\pm}_{p}=(\gamma_{\pm}N_{\mp}|C_{\gamma_{\pm}}|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}})^{-1}.

Furthermore, if
t_{p}=\min\big\{t^{\pm}_{p}\big\}\in(0,\infty)
, then

(4.8)

\displaystyle t_{p}=\begin{cases}\big(\gamma_{-}N_{+}|C_{\gamma_{-}}|^{1+\alpha_{+}}\big)^{-1}\quad&{\rm if}\ t^{-}_{p}\in(0,\infty)\ {\rm and}t^{+}_{p}=\infty,\\[2.84526pt]
\big(\gamma_{+}N_{-}|C_{\gamma_{+}}|^{1+\alpha_{-}}\big)^{-1}\quad&{\rm if}\ t^{+}_{p}\in(0,\infty)\ {\rm and}\ t^{-}_{p}=\infty,\\[2.84526pt]
\min\big\{\big(\gamma_{\pm}N_{\mp}|C_{\gamma_{\pm}}|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}}\big)^{-1}\big\}\quad&{\rm if}\ t^{+}_{p},t^{-}_{p}\in(0,\infty).\end{cases}

In the above,

\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
,
and
\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

are given by
(
3.2
)
–
(
3.4
)
.
See also
Fig.

4.1
.

Figure 4.1.
The five types of continuous shock generation points.

Proof.

By the arguments in
(
4.1
)
–
(
4.2
)
,
point
(x_{p},t_{p})

is a continuous shock generation point if and only if
(
4.2
)
holds.
From the definition of
\mathcal{U}(x,t)
in
(
2.5
)
,

\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
is equivalent to that

c
is the unique maximum point of
E(\cdot\,;x_{p},t_{p})
,
which, by
(
3.5
)
–
(
3.6
)
, implies that,
for all
l=t_{p}(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u))\neq 0
with
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\Phi(x_{0}+l)-\Phi(x_{0})-c\,l

\displaystyle>-t_{p}\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s=\frac{\int^{u}_{c}(s-c)f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s}{\int^{u}_{c}f^{\prime\prime}(s)\,{\rm d}s}\,l

\displaystyle=\big(\rho(u,c)-c\big)\,l=\big(\rho((f^{\prime})^{-1}(f^{\prime}(c)-l/t_{p}),c)-c\big)\,l.

From Theorem
3.12
,

t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
implies that
c\in\partial\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
.
There exist two cases:

(i)

For
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})<\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
there exist two subcases:

If
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
by Theorem
3.12
,
t^{+}_{p}=\infty

and
(
4.5
)
follows from
(
3.42
)
–
(
3.43
)
.

If
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
by Theorem
3.12
,
t^{-}_{p}=\infty

and
(
4.6
)
follows from
(
3.44
)
–
(
3.45
)
.

(ii)

For
\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,

(
4.7
)
–
(
4.8
)
follow from
(
3.46
)
–
(
3.47
)
immediately.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
∎

Corollary 4.2

(Formation of shocks for the flux functions of uniform convexity)
.

Suppose that
f^{\prime\prime}(u)>0
and
\varphi(x)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
A point
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
is a continuous shock generation point
if and only if there exists
x_{0}
on the
x
–axis with
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})<0
such that

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x=x_{p}=x_{0}+t_{p}f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0})),\\[5.69054pt]
t=t_{p}=\big(f^{\prime\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}))|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})|\big)^{-1},\end{cases}

and, for all
l=t_{p}(f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}))-f^{\prime}(u))\neq 0
with
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

(4.10)

\displaystyle\Phi(x_{0}+l)-\Phi(x_{0})-\varphi(x_{0})\,l>-|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})|\,k((f^{\prime})^{-1}(f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}))-l/t_{p}),\varphi(x_{0}))\,l^{2},

where

k(u,c)
is given by

\displaystyle k(u,c):=\dfrac{f^{\prime\prime}(c)\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime\prime}(c+\theta(u-c))\theta\,{\rm d}\theta}{\big(\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime\prime}(c+\theta(u-c))\,{\rm d}\theta\big)^{2}},\qquad\,\,\,\lim_{u\rightarrow c}k(u,c)=\frac{1}{2}.

In particular, if
f(u)=\frac{1}{2}u^{2}
, then
(
4.10
)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\Phi(x_{0}+l)-\Phi(x_{0})-\varphi(x_{0})\,l>-\frac{1}{2}|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})|\,l^{2}\qquad{\rm for\ any}\ l\neq 0.

Proof.

Since
f^{\prime\prime}(u)>0
and
\varphi(x)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
for any
x_{0}
with
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})\neq 0
,
then
\alpha_{\pm}\equiv 0
,
\gamma_{\pm}\equiv 1
,
N_{\pm}=f^{\prime\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}))>0
,
and
C_{\gamma_{\pm}}=\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})\neq 0
.
By
(
4.7
)
,
t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if
\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})<0
,
and it follows from
(
4.1
)
and
(
4.8
)
that
(
4.9
)
holds.
Finally, from
(
4.4
)
and
(
A.1
)
,

\displaystyle\rho((f^{\prime})^{-1}(f^{\prime}(c)-l/t_{p}),c)-c

\displaystyle=-\frac{l}{t_{p}}\,\frac{\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime\prime}(c+\theta(u-c))\theta\,{\rm d}\theta}{\big(\int^{1}_{0}f^{\prime\prime}(c+\theta(u-c))\,{\rm d}\theta\big)^{2}}=-|\varphi^{\prime}(x_{0})|k(u,c)\,l,

which, by taking
c=\varphi(x_{0})
, implies
(
4.10
)
.

In particular, if
f(u)=\frac{1}{2}u^{2}
,
then

f^{\prime\prime}(u)\equiv 1
so that

k(u,c)\equiv\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

For characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
with
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
if
(
4.3
)
holds,
by Theorem
4.1
,
t_{p}^{\pm}\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

C_{\gamma_{\pm}}<0
and
\gamma_{\pm}(1+\alpha_{\mp})=1
.
Thus, from
(
4.3
)
and
(
A.5
)
,
for small
\pm l>0
,

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)

\displaystyle=-{\rm sgn}(l)\frac{N_{\mp}+o(1)}{1+\alpha_{\mp}}|\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}}

\displaystyle=-{\rm sgn}(l)(1+o(1))\gamma_{\pm}N_{\mp}|C_{\gamma_{\pm}}|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}}\,|l|=-(1+o(1))\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}\,l,

where
\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}
are given by

(4.11)

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}:=\gamma_{\pm}N_{\mp}|C_{\gamma_{\pm}}|^{1+\alpha_{\mp}}=(t_{p}^{\pm})^{-1}.

We now present a necessary condition of continuous shock generation points,
which relays the further information of
f^{\prime\prime}(u)
near
c
and
\varphi(x)
near
x_{0}
as in
(
4.3
)
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume that
\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous on
\pm l\in(0,l_{0})
for some
l_{0}>0
,
and
(
4.3
)
holds for
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})

or
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
, respectively.
Suppose that
(x_{p},t_{p})
is a continuous shock generation point with
t_{p}=t_{p}^{\pm}\in(0,\infty)
.
If, for small
\pm l>0
,

(4.12)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)=-\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}l+\omega(l)l

for
\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}
in
(
4.11
)

and a continuous function
\omega(l)
possessing a unique zero point
\omega(0)=0
, then

(4.13)

\displaystyle\omega(l)>0\qquad{\rm for\ small}\ \pm l>0.

Proof.

Suppose that point
(x_{p},t_{p})
of characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0})
is a continuous shock generation point
determined by
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
with
t_{p}=t_{p}^{+}
,
or
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
with
t_{p}=t_{p}^{-}
, respectively.

For
(x,t_{p})
with
\pm l_{p}:=\pm(x-x_{p})>0
small,
by the Oleinik-type one-sided inequality
(
2.3
)
,

(4.14)

\pm l:=\pm\big(l_{p}-t_{p}(f^{\prime}(u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p}))-f^{\prime}(c))\big)\geq 0.

We claim that

(4.15)

\pm l>0\,\,\,\,\mbox{{\rm for} $\pm l_{p}>0$},\qquad\lim_{\pm l_{p}\rightarrow 0{+}}l=0,\qquad u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})=\varphi(x_{0}+l).

In fact, by continuity of solution
u=u(x,t)
at point
(x_{p},t_{p})
,

\lim_{\pm l_{p}\rightarrow 0{+}}u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})=c
so that,
by
(
4.14
)
,
\lim_{\pm l_{p}\rightarrow 0{+}}l=0
.
From
(
4.14
)
,

x_{0}+l=x_{0}+l_{p}+t_{p}\big(f^{\prime}(c)-f^{\prime}(u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p}))\big)=x-t_{p}f^{\prime}(u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})),

so that
(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})\in L_{u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})}(x_{0}+l)
,
which implies that

u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}+l)\neq\varnothing.

If there exists
\pm l_{p}>0
such that
l=0
, then, from
(
4.14
)
,

\pm\big(f^{\prime}(u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p}))-f^{\prime}(c)\big)=\pm l_{p}/t_{p}>0.

Since
f^{\prime}(u)
is strictly increasing,
then, for the case that
c=\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
with
t_{p}=t_{p}^{+}
,

\underline{{\rm D}}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=c<u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}),

which contradicts to
(
3.12
)
;
for the case that
c=\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
with
t_{p}=t_{p}^{-}
,

\overline{{\rm D}}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=c>u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}),

which contradicts to
(
3.12
)
.
Thus, we conclude that
\pm l>0
for
\pm l_{p}>0
.

Since
\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous on
\pm l\in(0,l_{0})
,

u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0}+l)\neq\varnothing
implies that

\mathcal{C}(x_{0}+l)=\{\varphi(x_{0}+l)\}
,
so that
u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p})=\varphi(x_{0}+l)
.

By
(
4.15
)
, it follows from
(
4.12
)
and
(
4.14
)
that

\displaystyle 0<\pm l_{p}

\displaystyle=\pm\big(l+t_{p}\big(f^{\prime}(u(x_{p}+l_{p},t_{p}))-f^{\prime}(c)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\pm\big(l+t_{p}\big(f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\pm\big(l-t^{\pm}_{p}\overline{C}_{\gamma_{\pm}}l+t^{\pm}_{p}\omega(l)l\big)=t^{\pm}_{p}\omega(l)(\pm l),

which implies that
\omega(l)>0
for small
\pm l>0
.
∎

4.2.
Development of shocks
near the five types of continuous shock generation points

From Theorem
3.14
,
by the arguments as for
(
4.1
)
–
(
4.2
)
,
for any characteristic
L_{c}(x_{0}):\,x=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(c)
,
point
(x_{p},t_{p})\in L_{c}(x_{0})
with
x_{p}=x_{0}+t_{p}f^{\prime}(c)

and
t_{p}
given by
(
3.36
)
–
(
3.37
)

is a continuous shock generation point if and only if

t_{p}\in(0,\infty)
and
\{c\}=\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})
.
There are three cases:

\displaystyle t_{p}^{+}=t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty),\qquad 0<t_{p}^{+}<t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty],\qquad 0<t_{p}^{-}<t_{p}^{+}\in(0,\infty].

The basic assumptions on the local behaviors of
f^{\prime\prime}(u)

and
\varphi(x_{0}+l)
near
c
and
x_{0}
respectively are presented as follows:
For simplicity, assume that, for some constants
\alpha\geq 0
and
N>0
,

(4.16)

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(u)=(N+o(1))|u-c|^{\alpha}\qquad{\rm as}\ u\rightarrow c.

Case I.

t_{p}^{+}=t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty)
.
Then
c=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
,
and it follows from
(
4.7
)
that

\gamma_{+}=\gamma_{-}
and
C_{\gamma_{+}}=C_{\gamma_{-}}
.
For simplicity, assume that there exists
l_{0}>0
such that

\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous for
l\in({-}l_{0},0)\cup(0,l_{0})
and

(4.17)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma}\qquad\mbox{as $l\rightarrow 0$},

where the constants satisfy that
C_{\gamma}<0
and
\gamma(1+\alpha)=1.

Furthermore, according to
(
4.12
)
–
(
4.13
)
,
assume that

(4.18)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)=-\overline{C}_{\gamma}\,l+\big(\overline{C}_{\sigma_{\pm}}+o(1)\big)\,{\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{1+\sigma}\qquad\mbox{as $l\rightarrow 0\pm$},

where the constants satisfy that

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{\gamma}:=\frac{1}{1+\alpha}N|C_{\gamma}|^{1+\alpha}=(t_{p})^{-1}>0,\qquad\overline{C}_{\sigma_{\pm}}>0,\qquad\sigma>0.

Moreover, if
\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}=\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}}=:\overline{C}_{\sigma}
,
assume that

(4.19)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)=-\overline{C}_{\gamma}\,l+\overline{C}_{\sigma}\,{\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{1+\sigma}+(\overline{C}_{\rho}+o(1))\,|l|^{1+\sigma+\rho}\qquad\mbox{ as $l\rightarrow 0$},

where the constants satisfy that

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{\gamma}=\frac{1}{1+\alpha}N|C_{\gamma}|^{1+\alpha}=(t_{p})^{-1}>0,\qquad\overline{C}_{\sigma}>0,\qquad\overline{C}_{\rho}\neq 0,\qquad\sigma,\rho>0.

Case II.

0<t_{p}^{+}<t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty]
.
Then
c=\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})\geq\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
.
For simplicity, assume that there exists
l_{0}>0
such that

\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous for
l\in(0,l_{0})
and

(4.20)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{+}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{+}}\qquad\mbox{ as $l\rightarrow 0{+}$},

where the constants satisfy that
C_{\gamma_{+}}<0
and
\gamma_{+}(1+\alpha)=1.

Furthermore, according to
(
4.12
)
–
(
4.13
)
,
assume that

(4.21)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)=-\overline{C}_{\gamma_{+}}\,l+\big(\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}+o(1)\big)\,{\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{1+\sigma_{+}}\qquad\mbox{as $l\rightarrow 0{+}$},

where the constants satisfy that

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{\gamma_{+}}=\frac{1}{1+\alpha}N|C_{\gamma_{+}}|^{1+\alpha}=(t_{p})^{-1}>0,\qquad\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}>0,\qquad\sigma_{+}>0.

Moreover, if
\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
,
assume that
\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous for
-l\in(0,l_{0})
and

(4.22)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{-}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{-}}\qquad{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{-},

where, by
t_{p}^{+}<t_{p}^{-}
, the constants
\gamma_{-}>0
and
C_{\gamma_{-}}\neq 0
satisfy that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C_{\gamma_{-}}>0\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{-}(1+\alpha)<1,\\
C_{\gamma_{-}}>C_{\gamma_{+}}\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{-}(1+\alpha)=1,\\
C_{\gamma_{-}}\neq 0\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{-}(1+\alpha)>1.\end{cases}

Case III.

0<t_{p}^{-}<t_{p}^{+}\in(0,\infty].

Then
c=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})\leq\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.
For simplicity, assume that there exists
l_{0}>0
such that

\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous for
-l\in(0,l_{0})
and

(4.24)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{-}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{-}}\qquad\mbox{ as $l\rightarrow 0{-}$},

where the constants satisfy that
C_{\gamma_{-}}<0
and
\gamma_{-}(1+\alpha)=1
.
Furthermore, according to
(
4.12
)
–
(
4.13
)
,
assume that

(4.25)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\varphi(x_{0}+l))-f^{\prime}(c)=-\overline{C}_{\gamma_{-}}\,l+\big(\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}}+o(1)\big)\,{\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{1+\sigma_{-}}\qquad\mbox{as $l\rightarrow 0{-}$},

where the constants satisfy

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{\gamma_{-}}=\frac{1}{1+\alpha}N|C_{\gamma_{-}}|^{1+\alpha}=(t_{p})^{-1}>0,\qquad\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}}>0,\qquad\sigma_{-}>0.

Moreover, if
\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
,
assume that
\varphi(x_{0}+l)
is continuous for
l\in(0,l_{0})
and

(4.26)

\displaystyle\varphi(x_{0}+l)-c=(C_{\gamma_{+}}+o(1)){\rm sgn}(l)|l|^{\gamma_{+}}\qquad{\rm as}\ l\rightarrow 0{+},

where, by
t_{p}^{-}<t_{p}^{+}
, the constants
\gamma_{+}>0
and
C_{\gamma_{+}}\neq 0
satisfy that

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C_{\gamma_{+}}>0\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{+}(1+\alpha)<1,\\
C_{\gamma_{+}}>C_{\gamma_{-}}\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{+}(1+\alpha)=1,\\
C_{\gamma_{+}}\neq 0\quad&{\rm if}\ \gamma_{+}(1+\alpha)>1.\end{cases}

Let
(x_{p},t_{p})
be a continuous shock generation point,
and let
x=x(t)
for
t\geq t_{p}
be the unique shock emitting from
(x_{p},t_{p})
.
Denote
u^{\pm}(t):=u(x(t)\pm,t)
. Since
\mathcal{U}(x_{p},t_{p})=\{c\}
,
it follows from
(
2.41
)
that
\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{p}{+}}u^{\pm}(t)=c
.
From
\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{p}{+}}x(t)=x_{p}=x_{0}+t_{p}f^{\prime}(c)
,

y^{\pm}(x(t),t):=x(t)-tf^{\prime}(u^{\pm}(t))\longrightarrow x_{0}\qquad{\rm as}\ t\rightarrow t_{p}{+},

so that there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

x_{0}-l_{0}<y^{-}(x(t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0}),t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0})<x_{0}<y^{+}(x(t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0}),t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0})<x_{0}+l_{0}.

Denote
(x_{\varepsilon_{0}},t_{\varepsilon_{0}}):=(x(t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0}),t_{p}+\varepsilon_{0})

and
x_{0}(t):=x_{0}+tf^{\prime}(c)
.
Let

(4.28)

\Omega_{\pm}:=\big\{(x,t)\in\Delta_{I}(x_{\varepsilon_{0}},t_{\varepsilon_{0}})\,:\,\pm(x-x_{0}(t))\geq 0\ {\rm if}\ t\leq t_{p},\,\,\,\pm(x-x(t))\geq 0\ {\rm if}\ t\geq t_{p}\big\},

where the
\Delta_{I}(x_{\varepsilon_{0}},t_{\varepsilon_{0}})
is the backward characteristic triangle of
point
(x_{\varepsilon_{0}},t_{\varepsilon_{0}})
,
i.e.,

\Delta_{I}(x_{\varepsilon_{0}},t_{\varepsilon_{0}}):=\big\{(x,t)\,:\,x_{\varepsilon_{0}}+(t-t_{\varepsilon_{0}})f^{\prime}(u(x_{\varepsilon_{0}}{-},t_{\varepsilon_{0}}))\leq x\leq x_{\varepsilon_{0}}+(t-t_{\varepsilon_{0}})f^{\prime}(u(x_{\varepsilon_{0}}{+},t_{\varepsilon_{0}}))\big\}.

See also Fig.
4.2
for the details.

Figure 4.2.
The three types of domains
\Omega_{\pm}
defined by
(
4.28
)
.

Theorem 4.4

(Development of shocks near continuous shock generation points)
.

Suppose that
(x_{p},t_{p})
determined by
(
3.36
)
and
(
4.1
)
,
as a point on the characteristic line
L_{c}(x_{0})

defined by
(
3.1
)
with
c\in\mathcal{C}(x_{0})
,
is a continuous shock generation point and
(
4.16
)
holds.
Let
x=x(t)
for
t>t_{p}
be the unique shock emitting from
(x_{p},t_{p})
.
Then the regularities of
both
the shock curve
x=x(t)
and the entropy solution
u=u(x,t)

near point
(x_{p},t_{p})
are as follows
:

Case I.

t_{p}^{+}=t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty)
.
Suppose that
(
4.17
)
–
(
4.19
)
hold
for
c=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})=\underline{D}_{+}\Phi(x_{0})
.

(i)

If
\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}\neq\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}}
,
then

(4.29)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad x(t)-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\cong{\rm sgn}(\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}-\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}})O_{1}(1)(t-t_{p})^{\frac{1+\sigma}{\sigma}}\quad\mbox{{\rm as} $t\rightarrow t_{p}{+}$};

and, for
(x,t)\in\Omega_{\pm}
near
(x_{p},t_{p})
,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad|u(x,t)-c|\lessapprox|C_{\gamma}|\bigg\{

\displaystyle(1-\delta)^{-\frac{\gamma}{\sigma}}\bigg(\frac{(\overline{C}_{\gamma})^{2}}{\overline{C}_{\sigma_{\pm}}}\bigg)^{\frac{\gamma}{\sigma}}\big|t-t_{p}\big|^{\frac{\gamma}{\sigma}}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\,+\delta^{-\frac{\gamma}{1+\sigma}}\bigg(\frac{\overline{C}_{\gamma}}{\overline{C}_{\sigma_{\pm}}}\bigg)^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\sigma}}\big|x-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\big|^{\frac{\gamma}{1+\sigma}}\bigg\}

hold for
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\delta>\max\{1{-}(Q_{\pm})^{-1}\}
.

(ii)

If
\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}=\overline{C}_{\sigma_{-}}
,
then

(4.31)

\displaystyle x(t)-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\cong{\rm sgn}(\overline{C}_{\rho})O_{2}(1)(t-t_{p})^{\frac{1+\sigma+\rho}{\sigma}}\quad\mbox{{\rm as} $t\rightarrow t_{p}{+}$};

and
(
4.30
)
with
\overline{C}_{\sigma_{\pm}}=\overline{C}_{\sigma}
holds
for
(x,t)\in\Omega_{-}\cup\Omega_{+}
near
(x_{p},t_{p})
with
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Case II.

0<t_{p}^{+}<t_{p}^{-}\in(0,\infty]
.

(
4.20
)

)
hold
for

.
Then

(4.32)

\displaystyle x(t)-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\cong-O^{+}_{3}(1)(t-t_{p})^{\frac{1+\sigma_{+}}{\sigma_{+}}}\qquad{\rm as}\ t\rightarrow t_{p}{+}.

For
(x,t)\in\Omega_{+}
near
(x_{p},t_{p})
,

(
4.30
)
with
\overline{C}_{\sigma_{+}}

holds for
\delta>1-(Q_{3}^{+})^{-1}
;

For
(x,t)\in\Omega_{-}
near
(x_{p},t_{p})
,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\qquad u(x,t)-c\cong\begin{cases}-C_{\gamma_{-}}(O^{-}_{4}(1))^{-\varrho}\,\big|x-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\big|^{\varrho}&{\rm for}\ a=c,\\[5.69054pt]
C_{\gamma_{+}}\big|x-x_{p}-(t-t_{p})f^{\prime}(c)\big|^{\gamma_{+}}&{\rm for}\ a<c,\end{cases}

where
\varrho=\max\{\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}\}
and
a:=\overline{D}_{-}\Phi(x_{0})
.

Case III.

0<t_{p}^{-}<t_{p}^{+}\in(0,\infty]
.

(

)
–
(
4.27
)

for
